After seeing them myself, I'm convinced WoW: Forever's RP servers don't need to be this separate, and I say that as a decorated veteran of MMO roleplay

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

I'm kinda just playing chiller PvE, here.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

I've been stuck into World of Warcraft: Forever—at the time of writing, I'm around 15 hours /played (and will likely be 20+ hours by the time you're reading this) on my warrior. I chose the roleplay ruleset because, well, I'm a roleplayer. I've been writing and being cringe in videogames for over 15 years.

RP in WoW is, actually, what got me into writing in the first place. My clear and obvious mastery over the written word was hard-won by being a terribly annoying edgelord death knight, for which I apologise.

I was curious about the implementation of the roleplay ruleset back when it was properly announced—back then, I thought it was great that we're being given our own place, but being completely split off from "normal" ruleset (which I'll be calling PvE for simplicity) didn't seem necessary to me.

Having played in the roleplay ruleset for a little while, my opinion has only been vindicated. Splitting PvE and PvP rulesets does make sense. For instance, a resource node might be easier to gather on PvE, throwing the economic balance of the PvP server out of the window. But beyond technical limitations, a complete, strict divide between RP and PvE just has me scratching my head.

There are things I like about having our own little nook of the Forever servers to putz about on. For starters, the vibes are a lot chiller. I don't want to cast generalisations about players, but I do think roleplayers are just smarter, sexier, have bigger muscles, and will solve climate change.

In seriousness, I do think thinking about your character's place in the world and having a bent towards immersion does make you a chiller person in general. I've experienced vanishingly little toxicity or hostility. The absolute worst instance was a tank getting a little passive-aggressive about mining nodes in my Deadmines run. That's it.

But what I foresaw is bearing out: Nobody is really roleplaying in instanced or group content, and that's because we legitimately never have done so. Most RP takes place either spontaneously out in the open world, or in dedicated hot spots. Even in the most diehard MMO communities, people aren't talking in ye olde English while pushing Vancleef's face in.

Keep up to date with the most important stories and the best deals, as picked by the PC Gamer team.

There are some people who are always in-character, sure, but that portion of the playerbase is incredibly rare. We're talking a sliver of an already-small population of a videogame, here.

As for the strict rules on naming conventions—yeah, I'm seeing a lot more lore-accurate names, but I'm also seeing plenty of joke ones. And if I'm being perfectly honest, these gag names really haven't ever bothered me; people being disruptive or annoying when I'm roleplaying are a massive pain, but if Tsundere Cowbell the paladin wants to watch me write, my immersion isn't shattered.

Overall, I think keeping us on our own server architecture for the most part is the smarter plan, especially given layering would dilute roleplayers so much that RP would be hard to find. However, I don't think there's any real reason to disallow us from jumping to the Normal ruleset servers when we group up with someone from there—or giving us an option to travel between them of our own accord.

Just as we tend to swap between in-character for roleplay and out-of-character for, y'know, playing the videogame—I think most of us would be happy to have the choice as to whether we want to futz about on the roleplay layer or go hang out with our non-theatre kid friends on the PvE ruleset.

Especially given, as time goes on, population might become an issue. Believe it or not, roleplayers still do like to raid and gear up, and if they should be able to migrate on characters they've invested literal dozens of hours into, rather than having to start all over again.

Harvey's history with games started when he first begged his parents for a World of Warcraft subscription aged 12, though he's since been cursed with Final Fantasy 14-brain and a huge crush on G'raha Tia. He made his start as a freelancer, writing for websites like Techradar, The Escapist, Dicebreaker, The Gamer, Into the Spine—and of course, PC Gamer. He'll sink his teeth into anything that looks interesting, though he has a soft spot for RPGs, soulslikes, roguelikes, deckbuilders, MMOs, and weird indie titles. He also plays a shelf load of TTRPGs in his offline time. Don't ask him what his favourite system is, he has too many.

SABO Project "softens divisions" inside Manhattan apartment

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

Photography by Alexandre Delaunay

New York and Paris design studio SABO Project has introduced rounded partitions and bold green accents to a compact apartment on Manhattan’s Lower East Side.

The 728-square-foot (68-square-metre) residence was renovated for a family of three with the help of contractor Alexar Interiors.

SABO Project opened up the plan and rounded the corners of walls and partitions between the various functional areas to visually improve the flow.

“The design aims to soften divisions between spaces so the apartment is experienced as a central living room extending into surrounding alcoves,” said the studio.

The apartment carries an industrial aesthetic thanks to exposed breeze-block walls, metal ductwork, concrete ceiling and polished concrete flooring.

Steel structural beams are painted a shade of mid green that is also applied to the lower kitchen cabinets, and appears on curtains and in artwork around the home.

“The apartment balances the rough materiality of exposed masonry blocks and ductwork with the soft textures of bespoke curtains, shades, and rugs,” said SABO Project. “Bold graphic forms animate curtains designed for each room.”

In the open-plan living area, a circular rug in the centre is mirrored on the ceiling by a white-painted circle that’s crossed with a minimalist light fixture.

Along one wall, stacks of concrete blocks with arch-shaped openings form a bench and a display console, accompanied at one end by a small wooden table.

“A long bench runs from the main room into a small outdoor space, conceived as an interior and enclosed with light-filtering fabric and a delicate steel mesh,” said the studio.

This small sun room is accessed via sliding glass doors from the living area and is also visible from the kitchen through a fixed glazed panel.

Here, green outdoor furniture from the Bouroullec brothers’ Palissade collection offers additional seating alongside the breeze-block bench.

Two identically sized bedrooms – one for the parents and another for their child – are decorated with the same colour and material palette as the rest of the apartment.

Quartets of breeze blocks are stacked on their sides to form nightstands with cubbies, and green appears again in the bedding.

In the bathroom, white tiles with green grout get progressively smaller as they reach the ceiling, creating a subtle gradient effect through density.

A lozenge-shaped mirror and sconce, and a basin with filleted corners continue the rounded forms found throughout the residence.

SABO Project was founded by architect Alexandre Delaunay, who works between New York and Paris on a range of residential, commercial and cultural projects.

The studio’s portfolio includes a Brooklyn loft featuring an irregular clustered storage unit, a Parisian apartment with multicoloured flooring and space-saving stairs, and another residence in the French capital that includes peg-hole walls and a curved child-proof staircase.

Photography by Alexandre Delaunay

Greenwashing Won’t Save You — How Gen Z Is Rewriting the Rules of Brand Authenticity

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

You're viewing the US website. Looking for the Site edition?

Discover how Gen Z is redefining brand authenticity, challenging greenwashing and demanding transparency, sustainability and real action.

Opinions expressed by Entrepreneur contributors are their own.

Walk into any corner store or scroll past a sponsored post, and you see it right away. Everything is suddenly “eco-friendly,” “sustainably sourced” or “carbon-neutral.” Companies slap a leaf icon on a plastic wrapper, paint the box olive green and call it a day.

They think this trick still works. It doesn’t.

If your marketing plan leans on empty claims to win over younger buyers, you’re playing a risky game. Greenwashing won’t save your startup. It might actually sink it faster than bad cash flow. Gen Z is rewriting the rules of brand honesty, and they have zero patience for corporate spin.

For two decades, big brands got away with soft marketing. Slap a vague promise about 2050 goals on a billboard, hire a PR agency to write a press release about planting trees and watch sales climb.

Gen Z grew up with a phone in their hand and a search bar at their fingertips. They don’t read your ad copy and take it at face value. They check your supply chain. They search your brand name on TikTok to see if employees are calling out bad workplace habits. They can even look up your parent company’s tax filings.

When a brand gets caught lying about its footprint, the backlash is fast, loud and it doesn’t go away. A fake green label doesn’t make you look responsible. It makes you look dishonest.

Young shoppers don’t expect your startup to be perfect. They know running a business takes resources and that shipping physical goods creates carbon output. What actually bothers them is the theater.

Take a small digital-first startup that sells modular workspace gear. Instead of hiding behind vague terms like “100% Earth-Friendly,” they list the exact components: 80% recycled aluminum, 20% new ABS plastic because of structural limits, made in a factory that runs on a grid with a high coal mix.

That kind of honesty instantly sets them apart from competitors shouting about saving the planet.

Gen Z wants hard facts over soft poetry. They’d rather buy from a business that admits where it falls short than one that pretends to have no flaws at all.

To win customer trust today, startups need to rethink how they talk to people. Here’s how honest brands are changing the story:

Show the receipts: Don’t just say your sourcing is ethical. Prove it. If you run a fast-casual food stall, put the actual invoices from local farm suppliers near your register or post them online every week.

Name the trade-offs: If recycled paper packaging doubles your unit cost and pushes up your retail price, just say so. Customers respect the honesty.

Stop waiting to be perfect: You don’t need a flawless environmental record to speak up. Share your current numbers today, set short-term goals for next month and report back when you fall short.

Modern shoppers know every purchase involves some kind of trade-off. When you pretend your product has zero impact, you insult their intelligence.

Take a modern specialty tea brand as an example. Instead of claiming their tea bags are fully biodegradable, when most actually need industrial composting facilities that regular city systems don’t have, they put a warning label right on the front of the pouch:

“These tea bags need commercial high-heat composting. If you toss them in your garden, they’ll take years to break down. Here’s how to dispose of them the right way.”

That one blunt line builds more brand trust than a million-dollar ad campaign built on fluffy green claims. It shows respect for the buyer’s intelligence.

If you want your business to survive this shift, you need to drop the corporate buzzwords and rebuild how you talk to people, starting now.

Cut words like “green,” “pure,” “natural” and “conscious” from your site unless you back them up with a specific third-party certification or a real number. If a claim can’t be checked, delete it.

Use your social channels to show operational hiccups. If a supplier fails to meet your material standards, post about the delay. Explain why you turned down the shipment. Turn your operational struggles into proof of your standards.

Big-budget, super-polished video ads read as dishonest to an audience raised on user-generated content. A quick, unedited founder video shot on a phone explaining a packaging decision lands far deeper than a slick commercial.

Gen Z isn’t asking every business to solve global problems on its own. They’re asking businesses to stop using those problems as a sales tactic.

If your product is good, let it stand on its own. If your operations are still a work in progress, show the process. Drop the green paint, publish your real numbers and talk to your customers like actual people.

Trust is not something you buy with a clever campaign anymore. It’s something you earn by showing up, dropping the spin and telling the whole truth every single time.

Walk into any corner store or scroll past a sponsored post, and you see it right away. Everything is suddenly “eco-friendly,” “sustainably sourced” or “carbon-neutral.” Companies slap a leaf icon on a plastic wrapper, paint the box olive green and call it a day.

They think this trick still works. It doesn’t.

If your marketing plan leans on empty claims to win over younger buyers, you’re playing a risky game. Greenwashing won’t save your startup. It might actually sink it faster than bad cash flow. Gen Z is rewriting the rules of brand honesty, and they have zero patience for corporate spin.

Solana’s October Upgrade Review Puts 200ms Slots and $5B in Tokenized Assets in Focus

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

Strict editorial policy that focuses on accuracy, relevance, and impartiality

Morbi pretium leo et nisl aliquam mollis. Quisque arcu lorem, ultricies quis pellentesque nec, ullamcorper eu odio.

TL;DR: Solana Foundation’s November 6 ecosystem review says two protocol changes went live in October: a reduction in target slot time to 200 milliseconds and Alpenglow consensus activation. It also reports a record $5 billion in real-world asset value. These are different metrics, and none alone proves a better experience for every application.

After a month filled with announcements, Solana has published a consolidated account of what changed on its network. The Foundation’s October 2026 ecosystem roundup, released November 6, links a pair of major performance upgrades with increased tokenized-asset activity and a growing roster of payment integrations.

The review is useful because it separates infrastructure that has activated from applications and partnerships still seeking wider use. It also offers a chance to examine how technical speed claims translate into financial operations.

According to the Foundation, a final stage of SIMD-0525 activated on October 9, reducing target slot times from the original 400 milliseconds to 200 milliseconds. Shorter slots aim to reduce the interval at which network leaders produce blocks. They do not guarantee that every transaction is confirmed within 200 milliseconds under all network conditions.

A second change followed on October 21 when Alpenglow replaced TowerBFT consensus on mainnet. The protocol targets roughly 150-millisecond finality. Finality describes when a transaction is sufficiently settled according to the network’s rules, and should not be confused with transaction submission, application response time or a merchant receiving funds in a bank account.

NewsBTC covered the Alpenglow activation earlier in the autumn. The new roundup does not make it a second launch. It provides a broader, dated view of how the two changes fit together, while reminding readers that production reliability has to be measured over time.

The Foundation reports that real-world assets on Solana reached $5 billion in value during October, alongside approximately $60 billion in decentralized-exchange trading volume and 14 million addresses holding stablecoins. These numbers measure different things. Asset value is not trading volume; a blockchain address is not necessarily a person; and gross turnover is not revenue to applications or validators.

The review includes new or expanded connections to traditional finance. J.P. Morgan Asset Management brought tokenized money market funds to the network through Morgan Money. The Foundation also describes stock-related products from Securitize and investment portfolios from Ondo, subject to their own eligibility conditions. For institutional users, the question is not only whether a token can move quickly but whether its legal ownership, redemption rights and custody arrangements remain clear.

That is also the theme behind Solana’s delivery-versus-payment programme, which seeks to coordinate an asset and its payment in one settlement process. Better throughput can help, but it cannot replace legal and operational safeguards.

The Foundation’s update lists stablecoin and remittance activity involving Samsung Wallet, Fiserv and Western Union, as well as integrations with business-payment services. Some products have launched, while other announcements refer to planned support, geographically limited access or commercial pilots. Those distinctions should survive any headline about ‘mainstream adoption’.

For developers and validators, the next meaningful evidence is sustained uptime, consistent finality under load, integration reliability and the cost of operating infrastructure. For investors, it is whether tokenized value corresponds to assets customers can actually redeem and use. Solana’s October was busy by the Foundation’s account; November’s challenge is showing the upgrades make routine financial activity more dependable, not merely more impressive on paper.

Why Project Hail Mary Author Andy Weir Thinks Sci-Fi Has 'Grown Up' Recently [Exclusive]

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

In an interview with /Film's Jeremy Mathai, Andy Weir, the author of the hit novels "The Martian" and "Project Hail Mary," recognized that the world has never been more accepting of sci-fi. What was once relegated to the realm of "nerds" and outsiders several generations ago is now embraced by everyone. And that, Weir noted, has allowed artists to tell more serious and dramatic sci-fi stories. When a mass audience takes it seriously, the genre, consequently, grows up.

And Weir has been doing his part to raise the genre's profile. The films based on his novels — Ridley Scott's "The Martian," and Phil Lord and Christopher Miller's "Project Hail Mary," to call them out by name — are known for their scientific rigor (especially "The Martian"). And although possessed of fantastical sci-fi conceits, both movies pay a lot of attention to science, research, and the power of human intelligence. These are movies for grown-ups that don't soft-pedal or water down the details of astronomy, physics, language, or botany. As such, these films come across as being comparatively "adult."

Weir also credited this in part to the various superhero films that've come out in the 21st Century. Many of those movies have sci-fi elements, and even a piece of Marvel fantasy fluff can bring more grown-up credibility to the genre. As Weir put it, "The superhero movie phenomenon helped bring more science fiction elements into the mainstream."

To be sure, artificial intelligence, parallel universes, and time travel frequently show up in superhero stories, and the genre's popularity has made a lot of those strange scientific theories a regular part of mainstream thinking. Yes, in that sense, even the box office smash-hit that was "Iron Man" helped the sci-fi genre grow up.

The recent boom of high-quality sci-fi television also came up during this discussion with Andy Weir. Just turn on Apple TV alone, and you'll find the cream of the crop in terms of modern sci-fi TV, be it "Severance," "Pluribus," "Foundation," or "Silo." Commenting on this, Weir noted:

"I think [the genre has] grown up a bit. So, it used to be science fiction was the bailiwick of just nerds. In the '80s, there were a few sci-fi shows. There was 'Star Trek: Next Generation' toward the end of the '80s. Then there was things like 'Quantum Leap,' and stuff like that. But other than that, the sci-fi shows that did exist were kind of cheesy. You got 'Battlestar Galactica' [...] It wasn't big mainstream entertainment."

Some "Battlestar Galactica" and "Quantum Leap" fans might bristle at these comments, but Weir is right on one point: sci-fi shows certainly seem more common nowadays. That, he argued, along with superhero movies and people now having access to more high-tech items, have further expanded their tolerance and devotion to the genre. As he put it:

[N]ow we've had a whole generation of people grow up in the computer age [...] [P]eople are a lot more socially accepting of science fiction as a legitimate setting [...] I love that there are serious, very well written, innovative and unique science fiction shows out there now."

Basically, sci-fi becomes real, essentially, sci-fi movies and TV shows become more normalized. And especially with all manner of sci-fi films having recently earned billions of dollars at the box office, it appears audiences, in general, are willing to give the genre more consideration, allowing sci-fi storytellers to take bigger risks in the process.

Connor Wood Reveals What Happened on 8th Avenue With Jennifer Lawrence in New Smoothie King Collab: “Missed My Moment” (Exclusive)

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

The 'Dancing With the Stars' contestant teamed up with Smoothie King to debut “Connor’s Paid Me No Mind Smoothie," a play on the trendy moment sparked by the Oscar winner.

Connor Wood is coming clean about what really led to Jennifer Lawrence‘s viral comment about seeing him on 8th avenue and him paying her no mind.

The Dancing With the Stars season 35 contestant teamed up with Smoothie King to debut “Connor’s Paid Me No Mind Smoothie,” which launched Thursday, a play on the trendy moment sparked by the Oscar winner.

Wood and Lawrence’s meta exchange occurred after he admitted she was his celebrity crush in an interview with BuzzFeed Celeb, and she commented, “I saw you on 8th avenue drinking a smoothie and you paid me no mind.” The Hunger Games star’s remark stunned the comedian, leading to a number of memes and a wave of virality, so much so that Wood collaborated with Smoothie King. Ocarina Of Time Remake Confirms Once And For All That ReDead Are Corpses

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

The Legend of Zelda: Ocarina of Time remake has confirmed whether or not ReDead are actually corpses, after Super Smash Bros. turned it into a fierce debate.

As anyone who has played Majora’s Mask can attest to, The Legend of Zelda can have some pretty strong horror chops when it wants to. Ocarina of Time is a great example of this, as its chilling ReDead enemies are infamous for being far creepier than anything else Link runs into during his quest, and arguably more than in any other game.

There are plenty of things that people were excited to see in the Ocarina of Time remake, but the ReDead were easily near the top of that list since everyone wanted to know whether Nintendo would make them as faithfully terrifying as before. Now that the game is out, we know that’s definitely the case, and that a misconception started by Smash Bros. is incorrect.

Over on Twitter, maliceguardian started a since-deleted thread about what the ReDead actually are. While most would say they’re simply zombies or mummies, maliceguardian pointed to the remake’s design for them (particularly the mask) as proof that they’re “clay golems shaped like humans”. If you’re confused about how that theory came about, it’s actually the fault of Super Smash Bros. and its trophies.

We finally got a chance to play The Legend of Zelda: Ocarina of Time remake and it's still one of the best games ever made.

In Melee, the ReDead trophy describes them as “just magic animated into hideous humanoid shapes”, while both the NTSC and Pal versions of Super Smash Bros. for Wii U and 3DS say they are “clay monsters fashioned in the shape of humans”. These trophies have caused many debates about whether ReDead are corpses, despite mainline Zelda games making it quite clear.

This clay monster description for the ReDead was adapted by Tri Force Heroes, which has them with a more plastic appearance and the ability to change shape.

After maliceguardian shared their thoughts on the matter, Twitter user VibeSharer put an end to the debate by pointing out that the Ocarina of Time remake’s Hall of Foes explicitly calls them “walking corpses with masks on their face”. As if that wasn’t concrete proof since it’s coming from the most recent Zelda game they appear in, AlTurkiei showed that you can even see their eyes behind the mask this time around.

I’m a little surprised that this was ever a debate, since Smash trophies aren’t exactly an encyclopedia of Nintendo canon and have been known to make mistakes in the past. At least we can finally put this long-standing misconception to rest and go back to being terrified of the ReDead instead of trying to explain them.

What’s It Like to Write Songs With Madonna, McCartney, and the Stones?

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

N o one has ever produced albums by both the Rolling Stones and Paul McCartney in the same year before, and those were just a couple of the highlights of Andrew Watt‘s last 12 months. Watt, who’s become music’s leading legend-whisperer, also continued his partnership with Lady Gaga on several new songs, worked on two tracks on Madonna‘s Confessions II, and produced Brandi Carlile’s Returning to Myself. His work with Michael Stipe on the soundtrack song “I Played the Fool’ led to an ongoing collaboration on Stipe’s upcoming solo album.

Watt talked about all of it and more in the latest episode of The Rolling Stone Studio. You can watch the whole conversation above, or read a lightly edited text version below.

Tell me about “Danceteria,” the song you-co-produced with Madonna, and the remix with Charli XCX.That song, in general, was just an amazing process to make. Talking with Madonna and hanging out with her, she has probably the coolest life of anyone. I’m from New York, so we just got into fun conversations about New York and, like, the scene and all the people that were around her. We started talking about Danceteria, which was the club where it all started for her.

As we were talking about the story of that place, it just became apparent that this should totally be a song. She would tell us, like, you look around and there’s Nile Rodgers and there’s David Byrne. And, like, all of the way that she was just talking about it seemed so cool, and she obviously invented talking on a pop song, right? And so it just felt so cool the way she was just naturally talking about it, so we threw the beat on, and then she started just talking about her life. It’s her origin story, and it’s amazing that it’s on a record, and you can listen to it whenever you want and hear all that. So I think Charli doing her [own] origin story was the cool thing for them being on it together.

I presume they did it separately. That’s how things go these days.Yeah, Charli recorded her part, but then they worked on the track and the production together, like, for a long time. We all kinda did, going back and forth. They’re both amazing producers. It’s a completely different record than the original. And I actually put them on a text message together to kinda meet and talk before they were ever gonna do a record together, because they just needed to be friends. They’re both so awesome and so cool, and really special people to me in my life. I was at a wedding with Charli, and I was like, “Have you ever met Madonna?” Editor’s picks The 250 Greatest Albums of the 21st Century So Far The 100 Best TV Episodes of All Time The 500 Greatest Albums of All Time 100 Best Movies of the 21st Century

And she’s like, “No, we don’t know each other.” And so I thought that needed to happen. And then the song was just a natural progression.

What synergy did you see in their production styles as they worked together on it?Well, they’re both extremely detailed producers. Know exactly what they wanna hear, how something makes them feel. Very technical. Like, turn this up a dB or down, or it needs to be a bit bright, the EQ needs to change here, or the compression’s too strong, or we need a synth here that’s like this, maybe something more of a stab instead of a bass sound.

It’s very, very detailed. It’s so much fun to work with an artist who also is a producer because they know what they want. Sometimes you can be talking about production with anyone that you’re collaborating with, and you have to figure out what they mean in emotional terms, which is your job, you know?

Or, “It makes me feel like this,” or, “I want to feel like that,” and what does that correlate to musically? It’s so awesome when someone’s like, “Actually, it needs to do this,” and they just tell you straight up in technical terms. And the music’s always gonna come out better and, I think, more personal when the artist is also the producer. Related Content Mick Jagger Praises Sam Fender’s Rolling Stones Cover Historic Brian Jones Letter Inviting Charlie Watts to Join Rolling Stones in 1962 Surfaces The Birth of ‘Confessions II’: Producer Stuart Price Breaks Down Madonna's Instant Classic The Grammy for Producer of the Year, Non-Classical, Will Go to the Best Storyteller

In general, what surprised you about Madonna’s vibe in the studio?I just loved sitting with her and watching her writing process. She likes to work in small spaces, which I thought was really cool. Watching her process and how detailed she gets with her lyrics and how she’d go home and write and then bring in more the next day.

Probably my favorite part of working with her, there’s this song that we did on the album called “Lower East Side Girl,” and I was just sitting with a guitar, and Stuart [Price] and Cirkut and Madonna were there. It was the four of us. But we kind of went downstairs in the studio while the guys were focused a little bit on the track and the production, and we just sat there with a guitar, and I was playing it, and we were kind of singing it back and forth and just writing lyrics back and forth.

And she’s such an amazing songwriter. And sometimes people like to write their lyrics alone, and she did some of that as well, but writing with her in person and going back and forth and seeing how her mind works was really beautiful. I think it started a friendship between us.

I have a feeling there must have been way more songs with that group of collaborators than just those two songs.Well, we didn’t work for very long, which was what was cool. I think we had four days in the studio. And we made three songs in the four days, two of which came out. So that’s pretty good. I think we’ll definitely work together again. We’re text buddies now and talk about art and different things, and I just absolutely loved the process of working with her, and whenever she needs me, I’m there.

You obviously have the most insane list of phone contacts. Do you keep people under code names just in case?Some people get a middle name of Fucking. So Mick Jagger is not Mick Jagger. It’s Mick Fucking Jagger, right? That’s his name.

What was the worst one?I butt-dialed Keith Richards’ house once, and he does not like the phone. I got called back and you gotta, like, prep yourself to like be on the phone with Keith. It was just calling me back, and it was like, “Hello, you called me.” And it was just weird. It lasted only about, like, two seconds, which most phone calls with him last about 10 seconds, so.

Now, most people love working with you. Tony Iommi said that he didn’t want to make the final Black Sabbath album that you wanted to make because — strange reason — you were too excited about it.Yeah. That, I think is taken out of context. You know me. I’m definitely an excited musician, a person.

That part I bought.Yeah, that would be a true statement. But that’s not why it didn’t happen. Ozzy was wanting to continue to work and make stuff. And we were making music, and he had the idea, ’cause we were talking about, “Let’s try and do something a little different than…” The two albums that we made, Ordinary Man and Patient Number 9, were different albums, but they were of the same DNA.

So we’re like, “What could we do that’s different? Do you wanna make something with Tony? Should we make an Osbourne-Iommi album?” We were talking about that, and then he was just like, “Let’s make a Sabbath album.” He’s like, “We didn’t do an album with Bill Ward.” I think looking back at all the stuff he wanted to do, making an album with Bill was really important to him, and this was before Back to the Beginning was happening and everything. I think the idea of doing something with Bill was very, very important. And so one of the first incarnations of that was a potential album. As Ozzy does, [he] gave me the marching orders, “Call Tony. Then you call Geezer. Then you call Bill.” Right? So called Tony. “Would you wanna do it?” “Yeah, that sounds cool, if Bill would do it, yes.”

Geezer, “If Bill would do it, yes.” It was all about, “If Bill would do it, yes.” So then I had to have a conversation with Bill, and that was one of the strangest and funniest and coolest conversations. It was a long conversation. He was speaking in tongues.

Which, now knowing him and being friends with him, makes sense. Long story short, and without going into it too deeply, he was unable to do the album for personal reasons. And there was a nice, beautiful, long e-mail sent to all of us, and I was on an e-mail with the original members of Black Sabbath, which was pretty cool.

Then after we talked, it was like, “OK, Bill can’t do it, and there’s no reason to do it.” I’m sure Tony didn’t feel like going into all of that. Tony’s awesome. He’s a great friend to me. He’s a great mentor. We made a couple songs together on Ozzy’s album and had an amazing time working together.

I was definitely excited about the fact that it was going and everything, but that wasn’t the reason why we didn’t make the album.

Fair enough.I think he’s just taking the piss. Which anyone that wants to do that with me, they absolutely should.

I’ve heard there is some unreleased Ozzy stuff. Is there anything that you can say about that?There are some songs that we made for a specific reason. And yeah, that’s all I can say. They’re hard for me to listen to. I haven’t listened to them.

I’m sure. Any plans for them that you know of?Kind of. Back to the Beginning, the night before the concert, I actually went in Birmingham with Sharon and a couple of people to go get a curry, and I was texting with Ozzy, who was in his hotel room, and he was like, “Come to the hotel, come to the hotel.”

I was sitting with Sharon, and we were both like, “He has a big day tomorrow. Like, maybe I shouldn’t go ’cause he’ll get excited and everything.” But he was like, “No, you’re coming to the hotel. You are coming.” So I went, I brought a curry with me, and sat with him, and he was drawing like he usually does, and we sat and we listened to the stuff that we had made, and made notes.

And that and the next day were the last times I got to see him. But we had a beautiful listening session and talked about a lot of stuff. It’s hard to think about, but I miss him every day.

I love what he told you about bass in rock & roll.I take it with me all the time. It’s really true. He asked me, “What’s the loudest thing in a Led Zeppelin album?” I just said, John Bonham. Ozzy said it’s the bass: “The most important part in a rock & roll mix is the bass.” He had so many things he taught me about.

And if Ozzy Osbourne chose you, he gave you a career. I mean, he did it over and over and over again. I always loved rock music, but I was a pop producer before he chose to let me stand next to him and work with him, and then I got to do all these amazing things after. But he was the first one, and he chose Randy Rhoads. ‘Cause he chose Zakk Wylde, Jake E. Lee, and then even you get to, like, now currently, Yungblud.

Like, Yungblud was on an amazing path before the Back to the Beginning concert, but Ozzy chose “Changes” for him to sing and loved Dom as a person and what he was doing as an artist and brought him out there, and he sang that song, and it changed his life.

You made a great song with Michael Stipe, “I Played the Fool,” and now you’re working on his new album. How did you end up with Stipe in the first place?We met through Elton John. We actually went to go see a play that Elton had done the music for, and we sat next to each other. Talked as much as you can during an intermission where you’re watching a play. We also had to be extremely attentive — we were both sitting right next to Elton, so we’re listening to every piece.

I was scoring the show Rooster, which is made by Bill Lawrence and Matt Tarses. Bill is my future father-in-law. I’m engaged to his daughter, Charlotte. And so we wanted to do something fun together, so I thought, “I’ll score one of your shows,” and this one really made sense, and it’s based around Steve Carell, and his character is a writer who comes to a college campus to kinda save his daughter, who’s going through some stuff, so he takes a guest-in-residence job. The sound of the show kinda became the musical sounds of bands that Steve, his character, Rooster, would’ve been listening to when he went to college — because he doesn’t go for this reason, but he ends up reliving his college experience.

When we were talking about the great Eighties American college bands, R.E.M. was the first one that came up, and both Bill and Matt’s favorite band is R.E.M., so there was a personal thing involved as well. So when that started happening, I was like, “I actually just met Michael this year, and so let me see if I can get in touch with him.”

So I asked Elton to put us in touch, and Michael says he answered because Elton’s never gave his phone number to anyone else ever. And so we met and hit it off and started working on this thing. And yeah, he’s become an awesome friend. I’m gonna see him later today, actually.

Michael has been working on a solo album for a really long time. It seems like he hasn’t wanted to get to the finish line. What is your sense of that?I don’t think that’s the case. He, as all great artists are, he’s extremely hard on himself. He works really hard at his craft, and he kinda beats himself up, I think, in a healthy way.

I’ve seen him write lyrics, throw them out, write them again, do all his own merit, you know? So I think he is wanting to make sure that he is gonna say exactly what he wants to say, and make a sound that’s exactly how he wants it to sound.

One of the things that he said is that he really misses — with R.E.M., they wrote beds of music, and he sang over them. So I think part of the challenge is to figure out what can substitute for that.Yeah, the key is he’s working with a bunch of different people, so… And it’s exactly that. Different flavors of different beds, and then finding his way around them, but he’s one of the all-time greats, great songwriter, great lyricist, great melodist. And really fun to be around.

The McCartney album is very adventurous in a lot of its choices. It’s very produced. In addition to some tight folky songs, there’s some great Wings-like guitar excursions, really letting him go out there. How much of that was his idea, and how much was you nudging him a little bit?Well, what was really cool about that record is it was just the two of us. Like, the whole time. So we’d just be bouncing off of each other. He is a hundred-percenter, which there are very few of. Can play every instrument, can mix, can produce, write every word, write every melody. Write all the chords. I mean, he does 100 percent of it.

His choice to have a producer is not out of necessity. It’s out of desire. So he likes to bounce off someone sometimes. I got the incredible honor of taking that role this time around. We would just have fun in the studio. Sometimes things would start from nowhere, which is not a common thing for him.

The way the Beatles would work, they would have songs that they’d write. They’d go over them at Paul’s place, which is around the corner from the studio, Abbey Road, and have a coffee and a cigarette in Paul’s garden, go over the songs, go over to the studio. There’d be one session in the morning for however long it was.

They’d take a break, and then they’d do the other session. I think they’re two, three-hour blocks or whatever. They’d take them both. They’d record two songs a day, and that’s how the Beatles made their music. His process is not, “Let’s go into the studio with nothing and see what happens” a lot.

And we got to do that here a little bit. So that’s how we ended up actually writing a couple of things together ’cause the guitars would come out, and we’d get on a thing. We’d get on a tangent and follow it down. There was never a point until, like, the last, like, two or three months in the five years that we spent working together where we were like, “OK, we’re making an album.”

It would just be like, “Hey, Andrew, I’m coming to L.A. at this time. Do you have a couple weeks to just kinda mess around?” And that’s what we would do. And we’d collect songs, and then we started working on some of the things that he had already started and everything, and then it kinda just turned into this natural, natural thing.

But working with him is like going to college. You know? Every time I’d get those couple weeks with him, I’d be a better musician, a better producer. I’d have more chord knowledge. Right after you work with Paul, you’re like, “OK, I’m ready to get right back in the studio” because, like, you’re better. You know? And then that eventually goes away because you’re not spending as much time with him.

When you have that incredibly rare privilege of sitting across from Paul McCartney and making a song from scratch, what do you learn about the essence of his creativity?His process is amazing. Him being around a collaborator is cool. His time alone is extremely important. Tucking away, often in a small space and writing, taking stuff home and writing in the morning. He’s fearless. And our whole collaboration started from, we were having tea. We were introduced by his manager, and he went to tell me, “You know, you can write a song out of anything.”

And he went to go grab a guitar. He’s like, “Oh, you only have righty guitars.” And I actually had some lefties brought over….

Just in case, right?Yeah. I woke up in a sweat the night before thinking, “What if he wants a guitar? What’s gonna happen?” I didn’t have any lefties. So—

But see, this is part of why you’re where you are, because you thought of that.I thought of it at the last second. Barely thought of it. Gave him a lefty. He played this weird chord, and then he’s kind of giggling to himself in a boyish way. It was such a weird chord that he had to resolve it. And once he resolved the chord, it was time to go to a third chord, and then the guitars came out, and we kind of—

And that’s the opening track of the album.That’s the opening track of the album. “As You Lie There.” And so that song was so much fun. Once that was there, it was kind of like, OK, let’s use the studio in all these fun ways, and he was kind of, like, teaching me the way he works. That was the point. It was like, you can write a song out of anything, and then sometimes you switch it up and go to a weird thing. It changes form a million different times, that song. And then it goes to a rock section. Then it goes to a bluesy guitar section. You know? It was kind of like, let me show you how we can use the studio. That’s how that song kinda came together.

And all of a sudden you realize why he’s the guy who made “Uncle Albert” and the Abbey Road medley and all this stuff.He loves playing characters, even, like, “Rocky Raccoon.” Who’s that guy? You know? He loves to play different characters and do voices, and his imagination is unlike anyone else’s. Like Mozart, you know? Bouncing around the studio and playing every different instrument, watching him do that, watching him know to play the cymbals quieter than the snare drum.

I asked him why he did that. He’s like, “So that you could crank the overheads, and it’s gonna sound great, and you’re not gonna be, you know, bashing people in the head with cymbals.” He’s thinking about all that stuff. Watching him arrange background vocals, which he does either at the piano or with a guitar in his hand at the mic, and goes through the note and sings it, and is really choosing the notes and arranging them.

He’s incredible at coming up with the perfect part on every different instrument and not making you feel like he played any instrument worse than the other. I mean, it just sounds like someone who’s a master on each instrument. Sounds like a band.

The Stones album is also incredible, in a very different way. Paul is kind of addressing mortality and looking back, whereas Mick and Keith and Ron sound immortal — libidinous and alive, as if they’re gonna go on forever. How do you even make sense of the amount of energy they bring?It’s a trippy thing to think about, right? Because for us, rock & roll’s been in our life forever, but in the history of music, it’s not that old of a genre. The Stones guys, they wanna rock. That’s what they wanna do. They want it to be loud, and get in the studio, and have drums, and push the tempos. And as with all bands that we love, it’s not about one person.

It’s the sum of all parts. You can listen to a Keith Richards solo album, they’re awesome. You listen to Mick’s solo albums, they’re awesome. Or Ronnie’s solo albums, there’s songs on there I love. When those three guys play together, the roof leaves the building.

It’s something that doesn’t sound like anything else in how they interpret each other and how they play off of each other. When I made those records, I would always be in the live room while the band tracked, kind of as far from Keith as I am from you, and Mick kinda right in front, and be in that circle so I could watch how they’re interplaying with each other and what they’re doing.

It helped me so much to understand, like, the chords and what they’re playing, and to be able to help arrange things as they were happening. And you don’t want it to ever be an arduous process with a band like that. They’re magic. If you’ve seen a Stones show, you see what happens when they hit a magic moment. It’s like, you wanna be able to help that happen as quick as possible when they’re playing a song. So my job would be to have a mic like this and call sections. “B section.” That one’s—

The conductor.Yeah, try and not get [my voice] into the overheads too much, and like, they kinda look up and switch, you know?

And, “OK, let’s go back to the A section. Take it out on that,” you know? And like, to make codas and stuff like that. So it would just be kind of, they’re playing the tune that they made and I’m arranging it as it’s happening in the moment.

Kind of amazing. If you had to guess, will there be another Stones album?I don’t know. I don’t know. I didn’t know if there was gonna be another one after the first one. If they call, I’m there. It’s the greatest honor of my life being around that band.

You’ve worked with so many great people, so many of your heroes. Where does that leave you as far as goals?I don’t ever really think like that. I’ve been so blessed to meet all these amazing people, and that turns into us working together. The artists that I’ve worked with has been a natural progression of meeting them and it making sense for us to work together. So I’m never really, like, going after someone or something like that. Things come into my life, and then I think about how amazing it would be to work with that person. But yeah, my antenna is open as always.

I think a lot of what you do is cross-pollinate. You’re bringing things from pop into rock, and vice versa, into K-pop, into whatever. You’re planting seeds, bringing sounds and people together. That seems like your overall thing.Yeah. I will say this, pop music is in a great place right now. You’re seeing the rise of all of these amazing pop girls coming back. It’s always been a big part of mainstream music, but there’s a lot of new artists breaking, and so that’s really exciting to me, too. I’ve been having fun making some pop music again, which I’m always doing as I’m making rock records as well. A part of the fun of my job is to get to work in all these different genres, but I’m really excited about what I’m hearing in pop music right now.

Your collaboration with Gaga is obviously ongoing. You did “The Dead Dance,” you did all those songs for The Devil Wears Prada 2. Tell me about those ongoing sessions. What’s it like to go back with her?Greatest feeling in the world is, like, when you make a project with an artist, it ends, and everyone’s sad, and you’re gonna miss each other, and then they call you back, and they’re like, “Let’s make more stuff.” You feel so great about yourself because that means they enjoyed working with you, they enjoyed the process.

She and I are from the same place. We’re from New York. We both grew up playing in the Bitter End, and were walking in the same streets, and in the same scenes a couple years apart. And so our foundation of music and our references are very aligned. So it feels like my sister, you know? Like my older sister, couple years older than me. When we sit, we break a song down, even when it’s an electronic song, and I’m on the guitar and she’s on the piano, sitting head-to-head and going through the song, it’s one of the most beautiful experiences of my life. It’s brought me to tears multiple times. It’s awesome.

Do you have a sense of when you’re working with her again?No. We’re in touch all the time, and it’s the same kinda thing. Whenever she sounds the bell, I’ll be there.

How do you work out the priority thing? What if Mick and Paul and Gaga all want you on the same day? What the hell do you do?It’s happened. You hold on, you breathe, and you figure out a way to make it all work. I find that if you are transparent always — it’s a big thing for me, transparency, especially in the music business. There’s so many people that are not that way.

And honest. You’re not lying to them.Yeah, that’s what I mean. People seem to react to that, and will follow you, and they’ll tell you when they really need you. And other people will be like, “Yeah, it’s OK. Well, let’s do it tomorrow,” whatever, you know? Or then there’s sometimes when everyone wants you at the same time, and then it gets fucked. But there’s always a way through it if you’re transparent and honest and real.

We were talking about cross-pollinating. What do you see in these veteran rock stars, and what do you see in the young pop stars you work with? What are the chief things you think they can learn from each other?They’re all different. And everyone works differently. And so you take something from that project, then you bring it into your next project. It’s just, like, natural. A great example of that for me was when I was working on the first Stones album, I had, like, a week off, and then we started the Pearl Jam album. And Keith had just showed me how to play in open G tuning and, like, gave me basically lessons and was like, “This is how you do it.” Trending Stories Michael Douglas Gives Update on Jack Nicholson: He's 'Well' But Has 'Disappeared' Appeals Court Says Lawsuit Over ‘The Pitt’ Can Proceed, Cites ‘Numerous’ Similarities to ‘ER’ As Nicki Minaj Rallied for Trump's GOP, Her Lawyers Told Court She Still Owes Them $295,000 Bob Dylan Meets With Pope Leo XIV in a Private Audience

And I started writing like that in my own way. And then he was, like, laughing. He was like, “You’re off to the races,” you know? And then I was able to take being able to play like that into Pearl Jam, and then there’s a song on the album that ended up in open G called “Wreckage,” that was directly influenced from learning open G tuning from the Stones.

And then that was just, like, a cool thing, you know? Learning how to record bands and capturing live energy is something that I try to bring into the pop music that I make. And the idea of performance-based music all the way through, not flying loops. That’s great, too. I do that all the time. Love doing that, but music that’s performed top-down does a different thing to you. So I try to incorporate that into the pop music and electronic music that I make as well.

Andrew Watt talks about working with the world’s biggest artists in an episode of The Rolling Stone Studio

Much-improved Newcastle stop the rot with a 1-1 draw at Fulham - but 'slapstick' defending and wasteful finishing will leave Matthias Jaissle rueing a missed opportunity, writes CRAIG HOPE

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

Published: 12:52 EDT, 7 November 2026 | Updated: 15:20 EDT, 7 November 2026

Just when Newcastle looked set for victory after Jacob Murphy had borrowed Amar Dedic's bow to arrow them into a late lead, they turned the weapon on themselves.

After a week in which Dedic, Bosnia's answer to Robin Hood, had made headlines when pursued by police for wandering around the Northumberland countryside with his hunting gear, the Geordies were ready to rob the good folk of west London after Murphy found the target ten minutes from time.

Less than three minutes later and they were picking the arrow from their own foot. William Osula failed to protect possession and allowed Fulham to enter the penalty area. Then, with the ball bouncing around the goalmouth, it was home substitute Rodrigo Muniz who pounced to punish Newcastle's scruffy defending.

'Slapstick', visiting boss Matthias Jaissle later called it.

The German also said that his team deserved to win but you got the impression he was relieved to emerge without defeat, especially as the performance was improved on the back of a run of four losses in five.

Newcastle's second-half spirit also showed a response to a week of noise centred on dressing-room unrest, not to mention Dedic's police drama.

Jacob Murphy thought he had won it for Newcastle after scoring late on at Craven Cottage

Rodrigo Muniz netted moments later after sloppy defending from Newcastle, earning a 1-1 draw

Matthias Jaissle's side were much improved but must cut out the errors to win matches

And Jaissle was right in that his team could have left with three points had master archer Harvey Barnes not missed a hat-trick of chances.

Fulham (4-2-3-1): Leno 7; Castagne 6 (Tete 77, 5), Affengruber 6, Bassey 6, Robinson 5; Berge 6, Charles 6; Bobb 6 (Sessegnon 78, 6), King 7 (Palacios 72, 5), Iwobi 6; Garcia 4 (Muniz 65, 8)

Subs not used: Lecomte, Andersen, Hugo Larsson, Manuel Angel, Kevin

Newcastle (4-2-3-1): Hornicek 7; Dedic 6, Thiaw 7 (Schar 85, 6), Burn 7, Hall 6; Nico Gonzalez 5 (Miley 61, 6), Steur 7 (Fernandez-Pardo 74, 5); Murphy 8, Wissa 6 (Willock 61, 5), Barnes 6; Osula 5 (Elanga 85)

Subs not used: Pope, Livramento, Botman, Bazoumana Toure

The winger is the player to whom they'd want those opportunities to fall, too. But twice before half-time, when denied by Bernd Leno from close range, and then again when lashing wide after the break, he failed to give his side the advantage.

Murphy corrected his team-mate's finishing when capitalising on a loose pass from Cesar Palacios and slamming into the bottom corner from 25 yards, but that joy was short-lived.

In truth, there hadn't been much joy to behold all afternoon. The draw keeps both sides in the bottom half of the table, and that is where they will likely stay.

The first half, in particular, had very strong last-on-Match-of-the-Day vibes.

Newcastle thought they had a 33rd-minute lead when Dan Burn stole in at the near post to flick home from Murphy's low cross.

What the officials had spotted at the far post was linebacker Yoane Wissa pinning defender Timothy Castagne, and so playing Burn onside. It was the right call, even if Jaissle disagreed

'I learned that word here, a 'soft' decision. In my eyes, it was not right,' he said. 'But I was pleased with our complete commitment and togetherness.

'We grew through the game and that was excellent, something to build on. We showed the fundamentals.'

Dedic twice came close to creating goals with teasing crosses from the right, and you wondered if he would have mimicked Barnes' trademark archer celebration had they been converted.

He was kept away from the media post-match, but Jaissle did say: 'He played well, right? It (the bow & arrow incident) didn't affect him. He was not aware of the rules. We don't need to make a big deal out of it.'

Fulham's Josh King was included in Thomas Tuchel's England squad on Friday, the club's first Three Lions call-up in eight years. He, however, now needs a scan on a tight groin before learning if he's fit enough to feature.

Did the 19-year-old play like a would-be England international? He was his side's best player in the first half, even if that was a low bar to hurdle. One volley would have raised the roof had it gone in, so sweet was the connection from 20 yards. Lukas Hornicek needed every inch of his 6ft 6in frame to dive and save. And that, too, was a recurring theme, Newcastle's reliance on the big Czech to bail them out when giving up chances.

Like Newcastle, however, Fulham simply weren't good enough to take them.

Arsenal 1-0 Hull City: Kai Havertz sets extraordinary offside landmark as Martin Odegaard wins close Premier League match

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

By Lewis Nolan | 07 Nov 2026 17:49

Kai Havertz has been caught offside more times this season in the Premier League (15) than Bukayo Saka was in the entirety of last season (14).

The Gunners striker was controversially pulled back in the penalty area in the first half against Hull City on Saturday afternoon, with the forward expecting a penalty.

Despite referee Farai Hallam was sent over to look at the incident by VAR, though an apparent offside offence saw Mikel Arteta's hopes of claiming a penalty waved away.

While there was controversy about the decision for Havertz's offside position to take precedence over the foul, the fact that the German was ahead of play was concerning.

Opta data shows that the forward has been caught offside more in the league this season (15) than Saka was in 2025-26.

15 - Kai Havertz has been caught offside 15 times in the Premier League this season, already more than any Arsenal player was flagged in the competition last term (Bukayo Saka, 14). Strayed. https://t.co/QkneG5oDNh

Havertz should not necessarily be criticised for trying to make runs in behind defences, but he is not suited to that role in the team.

Arsenal substituted Viktor Gyokeres on for the 27-year-old in the second half, and while the Swede often makes selfless runs, his return in the final third has been poor.

In fact, the two strikers have only scored three goals between them in the Premier League this season, and they have produced just one assist.

Similar concerns were raised about the pair in 2025-26, and it has become clear that neither should be Arteta's starting nine next season.

Though Arsenal's forwards have rarely offered much of a threat in the final third, there is little reason to panic given they have won nine of their opening 10 league games and have looked exceptional at the back.

Arteta's side have kept seven clean sheets already, and they proved last season that they do not need to be perfect in attack to win a league title.

While their forward line may let them down at times this campaign, there is no reason to think any of Arsenal's possible rivals will catch them.

The double Daniel Farke masterstroke that saw Leeds sink resurgent Tottenham

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

Please refresh the page or navigate to another page on the site to be automatically logged inPlease refresh your browser to be logged in

Leeds 1-0 Tottenham: Harry Wilson and Noah Okafor combined 97 seconds after coming off the bench to produce the winner at Elland Road

Want to bookmark your favourite articles and stories to read or reference later? Start your Independent Membership today.

There have been times in his reign at Elland Road when the accusation has been that Daniel Farke leaves it too late to make his changes. Call it a lack of proactiveness, perhaps, but there are days when the patient approach pays off. After the impasse came an instant impact. After the long wait came the speediest goal by a substitute in the Premier League all season.

Leeds’ goal drought was ticking up towards the five-hour mark; with an international break coming up, they were set to go a month without finding the net, and all their manager had done was to swap his left-backs at half-time, and even then only because Gabriel Gudmundsson had a hamstring problem.

After 77 minutes, Farke belatedly summoned Harry Wilson and Noah Okafor. Some 97 seconds later, they combined to clinical effect. From the possibility of a third stalemate of the season, Leeds had the reality of a first victory in almost eight weeks, since the heady night they demolished Newcastle to go third.

Another of the questions surrounding Leeds was if they had enough strength in depth in attack. They had a lone goal contribution from a substitute this season, in Lukas Nmecha’s assist against Brentford; a consequence, some may say, of delaying changes. In an instant, that tally was trebled.

The concern remains that they are over-reliant on Dominic Calvert-Lewin for goals. A winner that did not involve their top scorer helped address that. In its various ways, it was a win and a winner to offer reassurance that Leeds are on the right track.

“You get sent on as a sub at 0-0 and you are just trying to look for that moment,” Wilson said. He laid the ball off for Okafor to drive into the box and power a shot past Antonin Kinsky. They were his second and third touches of the ball. “When it fell to Noah, it was a fantastic first touch and finish,” Wilson added. A third replacement had played a part, with Melvin Bard, introduced at half-time, taking the throw that Jan Paul van Hecke inadvertently flicked on to Wilson.

There was a significance for the Welshman. It was a first goal involvement in the Premier League for Wilson since his move from Fulham. Leeds had devoted much of their summer spending to bolstering the defence, in the shape of the £40m club record signing of James Trafford and the £35m defender Tarik Muharemovic. Yet it was done with the feeling that they had acquired an attacking talent worth around £25m in Wilson, recruited on a free transfer when his contract at Fulham expired.

After 10 goals and seven assists for Fulham last season, this had been an underwhelming start, but Farke felt his best game for his new club had come at Bournemouth last week. It had been hard to leave him out, he said.

But he then found it hard to bring him on. Farke was slow to act partly for tactical reasons, partly for physical. He held Wilson back because he wanted a third central midfielder, in Sean Longstaff. The medical staff had told him that Okafor, who has been struggling with an adductor problem, could only play 15 or 20 minutes. “You must be unbelievably disciplined,” Farke said.

There was another explanation for his delaying tactic. Leeds had been gathering momentum in the second half. In a tale of two Dominics, Calvert-Lewin had been denied by a goalline clearance from Solanke. Nico Elvedi had headed wide; Ao Tanaka spooned a shot over; Sean Longstaff was denied by Kinsky. Pressure was building.

It was a tale of two right-backs, too. After a lengthy VAR review, Jayden Bogle escaped a red card for catching Mathys Tel’s heel with his studs, causing the Tottenham winger to hobble off, leaving Roberto De Zerbi fearing a “bad injury” and Farke defending his defender. “Jayden Bogle is one of the most fair players in the country,” he said. “I always call him my butterfly, and I can guarantee it was never out of bad intention.” Pedro Porro, too, was spared a sending off, though correctly, having already been cautioned when he tripped Nmecha. De Zerbi promptly hauled the Spaniard off.

The Italian was honest in his appraisal, blaming his side more than the officials. “Second half, we deserved to lose,” he said. “We lost all the duels and didn’t play like we can. The problem is we did not show character, we did not show personality, we did not show courage.”

After winning their previous three games, Spurs did too little to win this. Trafford had given a goal away against Bournemouth last week. He was largely untested by Spurs until injury time, when he tipped Van Hecke’s header away. That the centre-back is Tottenham’s top scorer in the Premier League is an indictment.

So was their second half. For the first time, Spurs were wearing a purple third-choice kit. It was more a case of purple pain than purple reign.

Khadija Shaw hat-trick powers Manchester City past West Ham 5-0 in the WSL

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

To help deliver timely coverage of women's football, we may use AI tools to support content creation and editing. While we strive for accuracy and editorial quality, readers should independently verify important information.

Manchester City beat West Ham 5-0 in the WSL at the Manchester Regional Arena on Saturday 7 November, scoring four times after the break to turn a narrow half-time lead into a rout. Khadija Shaw scored three, with Aoba Fujino and Iman Beney also on the scoresheet.

Rita Guarino’s side held out until the 38th minute. Carlotta Wamser then supplied the pass for Shaw to open the scoring, and it stayed 1-0 at the interval.

The second half was a different story. Shaw doubled the lead in the 53rd minute from a Lauren Hemp assist, and Fujino added a third seven minutes later. Fujino then turned provider for Beney in the 68th minute.

Shaw finished the job in the 74th minute. Grace Clinton set her up. That made four goals in 21 minutes after the restart, and Shaw ended with a match rating of 9.66, the highest on the pitch, from six shots in 75 minutes. Fujino, with a goal and an assist, was rated 8, while Hemp created five chances.

City had 10 shots on target to West Ham’s 2, and 55% of the ball. Their expected goals figure was 2.14, well short of the five they scored. West Ham managed 13 attempts but only twice tested Yamashita, who made 2 saves, while Picaud was kept busy with 5 at the other end.

Referee Megan Wilson booked Rebecca Knaak and Kerstin Casparij for City, and Viviane Asseyi for West Ham in first-half stoppage time. Asseyi, with 5 league goals, sits level with Hemp in the scoring charts.

Shaw’s treble took her to 9 WSL goals for the season. Pelova of Tottenham Hotspur and Malard of Chelsea are next on 6 apiece.

City are top with 25 points from 9 games: eight wins, one draw and no defeats, with a goal difference of 17. Chelsea are second on 23 points from the same number of matches, while London City Lionesses are third on 16. Andrée Alexander Jeglertz’s side are unbeaten in six matches in all competitions, a run that includes a 4-2 win over Arsenal and draws with Manchester United and Paris Saint Germain.

This was also a fourth successive win over West Ham, after a 1-0 league victory in November 2025, a 5-1 WSL Cup win that December and a 4-1 win in May. The last West Ham result in the fixture that was not a defeat was a 1-1 draw in March 2025.

For West Ham, the defeat ended a two-match winning run that had featured a 4-1 victory over Liverpool. They are 10th with 9 points from 9 matches and a goal difference of minus 10.

City switch to the Champions League next, away to Inter Milano on Tuesday 10 November. West Ham host Charlton Athletic in the WSL on Sunday 15 November, against a side without a point after 8 games.

iPhone Air 2 is coming next year, here’s why it has more to prove than ever

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

Next spring, Apple will take the stage to unveil the second-generation iPhone Air to the world, after taking a bit more time to go back to the drawing board and incorporate a second camera into the design. That said, iPhone Air is no longer the flashy cool phone, now iPhone Duo is.

With that being the case, it seems obvious to me that iPhone Air 2 will need to appeal to a wider audience, beyond just the enthusiast crowd.

iPhone Air wasn’t an incredible seller. That said, it was still an incredible marvel of engineering – proving that you can still pack a largely flagship smartphone experience in such a thin design. It wasn’t perfect though, and the compromises alongside its higher price tag meant it only appealed to a small audience.

Now that iPhone Air 2 won’t have the shiny-new-thing factor going for it, it seems almost imperative for Apple to address some of the key shortcomings of the original iPhone Air.

We already know Apple is going to address two of the biggest ones: adding a second camera, as well as a larger battery capacity.

iPhone Air concerns don’t end there, though. There’s also compromised speakers, a compromised connectivity and charging experience, and a compromised camera experience.

iPhone Air has a single mono earpiece speaker. Audio on it isn’t excellent. Luckily, with iPad mini 8, Apple introduced acoustic display – which allows speakers to play through the display glass. Perhaps some of that tech can be taken for iPhone Air 2 to improve the subpar speaker experience.

Another major drawback is connectivity and charging. iPhone Air only supports USB 2, while the Pro iPhones support USB 3. Does this matter to most consumers? Maybe not. When the iPhone Air fetches very close to Pro pricing though, it should offer it. On the charging front of things, iPhone Air only supports 20W MagSafe and 25W fast charging, compared to 25W MagSafe and 60W fast charging on iPhone 18 Pro.

Lastly, the camera. I expect the camera quality to still be limited as a result of smaller sensors, but there are a few things you miss out on with iPhone Air: RAW support, cinematic mode, and macro mode. The latter is more of a result of the phone lacking an ultrawide camera, so ideally we’ll see macro come to iPhone Air 2.

All in all, iPhone Air 2 needs to have a firm position in the lineup. If it’s going to come within $100 of a Pro iPhone, it should offer an experience close to that. The fact that it’s thin should be just another feature, rather than it being a justification for compromise.

Wendy’s Just Released a Video Game Where You Can Win Fast Food

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

Our gifting experts are obsessively searching for holiday gifts that are unique, thoughtful and suitable for any budget.

Our expert, award-winning staff selects the products we cover and rigorously researches and tests our top picks. If you buy through our links, we may earn a commission.

I won a medium fries by defeating a sinister robot that looked suspiciously similar to a McDonald’s french fry container.

Wendy’s has turned its red-headed mascot into a wisecracking warrior who battles evil french fry robots in a free mobile game called WendyQuest. The company announced the retro-style role-playing game on Nov. 4. The announcement came with a promotion where you can win codes in the game, which are redeemable for menu items like fries and burgers at Wendy’s locations.

WendyQuest is available to download on iOS and Android. Desktop users can get it through the Steam platform. Anyone can play the game, but the promotion is restricted to people who are 18 and up living in the US.

You need to sign up for a Wendy’s account to redeem a code. While the food you can win is free, it requires you to buy something when you go.

“Wendy’s has been a part of gaming culture for years, including on Twitch, Fortnite and Feast of Legends, but WendyQuest is the brand’s first fully owned video game,” a spokesperson for Wendy’s said.

In 2019, Wendy’s partnered with the online multiplayer video game Fortnite, as well as the video-streaming platform Twitch, for various promotions featuring the company’s mascot. The company even released a rule book for a traditional tabletop roleplaying game the same year, called Feast of Legends, as a free download.

I downloaded WendyQuest, and my daughter and I played it for a while. The blocky graphics are reminiscent of the home-console video games of the late 1980s and early 1990s. It plays a lot like a simplified version of the DragonQuest, Final Fantasy and Breath of Fire games from the era, only stuffed with fast-food branding.

Rather than the regular turn-based battles that come standard in the early RPGs, this game adds a timing element, where you need to press a button at the right time like you do in old golfing video games.

It’s not a bad game. The light humor is suitable for younger players, and it’s fun to play. Like many RPGs, you battle monsters to gain levels, search around the maps for hidden areas, buy items and talk to people in towns to figure out what’s going on.

It took us over an hour to complete the first region, Fryshire, and get the Fry Stone, which unlocks a promo code for medium fries, free with a purchase. But again, you have to sign up for a Wendy’s account on their website or on the company’s mobile app.

Video games have a long history of offering real-world prizes to players as promotions. Swordquest, a series of video games released in the early 1980s for the Atari 2600, famously held a contest where players tried to win actual treasure made of gold and jewels.

In 2015, a promotion for the video game Just Cause 3 offered players the chance to win a private island. Developer Square Enix clarified that it couldn’t guarantee that the island would be livable and that the winner would have to pay all the taxes involved.

The reward codes from WendyQuest expire seven days after you get them, and Wendy’s says the codes are first come, first served.

Kate Middleton Dresses Down for Appearance at Mental Health-Focused Charity

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

During a surprise engagement in West London, Kate Middleton reminded fans that she is the queen of country-set dressing. Prince William's wife joined the team at Dose of Nature, a charity that “prescribes” time spent outdoors to help improve mental health, telling staff members and volunteers that connecting with nature is a “healing” experience for her.

The future queen dressed down for the occasion, wrapping up warm in what looked to be a Barbour jacket and a pair of jeans. She left her hair to flow in waves and accessorized with her trademark “big blue” sapphire engagement ring.

At Pensford Field, where Dose of Nature has a site near Kew, the Princess of Wales met the charity's founder, Dr. Alison Greenwood, and clinical lead, Dr. Georgina Gould, as well as volunteer nature guide Tom, who told Catherine that support from Dose of Nature had made him more confident and resilient.

“Our journey has been very very different, but it’s so wonderful to hear how your nature connection and how it’s been so healing for you, and I felt the same,” Catherine said to him.

“The fact that you’re able to provide that to so many other people in this community; that sense of purpose and meaning, and belonging, through that experience, I think is something that has got the longevity and the sustainability of feeling like, almost without knowing what’s to come, without having the control in your life of knowing what the future might hold, the fact that you feel that you can cope with things, because you feel that interconnection, I think that makes it holistic in a very true essence,” she added.

The Princess of Wales visits Dose of Nature.

Kate Middleton has long been a champion of the importance of nature for mental and physical well-being. In a recent essay, she wrote: “By spending time in nature or being creative, we can nurture the very skills and emotions that cannot be digitised: awareness, empathy, humility, and above all, love.”

The future queen has been working with the Royal Foundation Centre for Early Childhood on a project to “support the healthy development of babies and young children,” as she explained in a recent video, delivered from Cornwall while dressed in ME+EM.

“In this tech-driven, digital age, spending time outdoors has never been more important,” the Princess said.

“This project is about how nature, creativity, and relationships can best support the healthy development of babies and young children,” she continued. “My hope is that through this practical, nature-based research, we can build supportive environments that help make nature more accessible, benefiting babies, young children, and their families across the UK.”

The Princess of Wales has spent many years dedicated to early childhood research, working hard advising the Foundation, even during her cancer treatment. This May, she visited the University of East London’s Institute for the Science of Early Years and Youth to see how the University’s researchers use state-of-the-art wearables and brain recording techniques to study how the diverse early living environments and relationships experienced by babies influence early stress—both in the child themselves and their parents and caregivers.
