The UV-tracking Gem made me rethink how sunlight affects my health, not just my appearance

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

What started as a test to understand how sun exposure was aging my skin took a turn that I wasn’t expecting.

What started as a test to understand how sun exposure was aging my skin took a turn that I wasn’t expecting.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

With temperatures rising to the 90s in Los Angeles, avoiding the heat wasn’t exactly an option while I was at a relative’s outdoor party in August. So I sat beneath a patio umbrella with a powerful fan nearby, figuring I was protected from the sun and heat. Half an hour later, I felt exhausted, dizzy, nauseous, and generally unwell, symptoms consistent with heat exhaustion.

Around the same time, I received a notification from an app used by the $249 ($149 at the time of publication) UV-tracking necklace I’d been wearing, called Gem, from a company called The90. It warned that my UV exposure had climbed considerably. It turns out UV radiation wasn’t repelled by this patio umbrella, and it’s hard to ever be fully protected because radiation can be reflected or scattered by objects in the environment. While UV exposure and overheating are different things, I’d mistakenly assumed the shade and the fan would adequately protect me from both.

This led to a discovery: One of my medications increases my sensitivity to heat, which helped to explain similar episodes I’d experienced over the years. The Gem didn’t diagnose anything, but it prompted me to investigate something I might otherwise have dismissed. Since then, I’ve been more mindful about limiting time outdoors during hours of peak heat and UV, staying hydrated, and paying closer attention to how I’m feeling outside.

I’ll admit my initial interest in tracking UV was largely vanity-driven. As much as I try to challenge harmful beauty standards, I’m hardly immune to them. Having started going gray in my 20s, I’m keen on slowing down other visible signs of aging where I reasonably can. But between being Indian (I rarely get sunburned thanks in part to my skin’s higher melanin content, not that it eliminates the risk of sun damage or skin cancer) and living within walking distance of the beach in perpetually sunny LA, I hadn’t given much thought to how much UV exposure I was actually getting. The Gem has helped me better understand it by providing a range of estimates, some more useful than others.

The included UV-detecting sensor measures UVA and UVB radiation separately, rather than simply displaying my area’s UV forecast. UVB is primarily responsible for sunburn and helps your skin produce vitamin D, while UVA contributes to premature aging, even when you aren’t visibly burning. Thankfully, getting these metrics doesn’t come at the cost of wearing a bulky device. The circular pendant comes with a cute, thin necklace I’d happily wear even if it didn’t track anything. Its titanium, gold- or silver-colored chain pairs well with a variety of outfits. Battery life lasted almost exactly a week (the advertised lifespan), and the Gem recharges using an included charging dock. The90 plans to release a hat clip and bag tag early next year, which seem ideal for those who prefer not to wear it around their neck.

The mobile app is central to the experience. It has easy-to-read graphs and jargon-light explanations. I enjoyed using it. While not required, you can create a skin profile so that it can estimate a range of metrics based on the sensor’s UVA and UVB readings. The profile is based upon factors like skin type, sensitivities, clothing, and sunscreen. That means its estimates aren’t based on UV exposure alone, though they’re still predictions rather than direct measurements of how your body is responding to sunlight.

There’s a lot here, including your estimated vitamin D production, potential circadian light benefits, estimated nitric oxide-related benefits, cumulative UVA exposure, and recent accumulated UVA exposure. Alongside cumulative UVA exposure and weekly trends, its Skin Aging Zone assesses potential aging-related risk and offers personalized skincare advice. It also provides a personalized sunburn countdown, sunscreen reminders, and a daily Sun Score.

The Sun Score is a rating that incorporates various metrics to balance sunlight’s potential benefits against the risks of overexposure. The company encourages users to aim for a daily Sun Score of 90, though more sun isn’t necessarily better. Too little exposure means missing potential benefits, while excessive UV can damage your skin. I appreciated the context, but the individual estimated measurements proved more revealing than the Sun Score.

The UV readings in the app responded quickly as I moved between sunlight, shade, and indoors, even registering changes near windows. I couldn’t independently verify the sensor’s accuracy, but the readings behaved as expected as I shifted between environments. However, there were times when I noticed the Gem sometimes failed to register that I had been outside. Eventually, I discovered the app needs to remain running in the background (at least on iPhone; I haven’t tested on Android), as measurements aren’t stored on the sensor, and fully closing the app interrupts tracking.

My experience at the outdoor party challenged my assumptions about how much protection I was getting from the shade. Just as surprising to me was learning how little UVB I accumulated during a typical week, despite living somewhere so sunny. I fell short of the app’s estimated sunlight-derived vitamin D target almost daily, prompting me to research how melanin, while offering some natural UV protection, impacts vitamin D production from sunlight. Until then, I’d mostly thought about sun exposure in terms of protecting my skin from damage. Seeing how little UVB I was accumulating made me consider what I might be missing out on, too.

The vitamin D countdown wasn’t as helpful as I had hoped. During one test, its estimate of how much additional sunlight I needed dropped from several hours to under an hour, then climbed past six hours just minutes later. The fluctuations appeared related to changing UVB intensity, though I couldn’t verify that. I noticed similar fluctuations in the app’s estimated time to sunburn. With both countdowns changing so dramatically, I stopped paying much attention to the exact times and focused instead on the broader picture: whether it felt like I was getting too much or too little sun.

That was perhaps the biggest surprise of testing the Gem. After my experience using it at the outdoor party in August, I’d expected it just would encourage me to spend less time in the sun each day. In reality, my exposure often falls at one of two extremes: either too little to meet the app’s estimated targets or enough to raise concerns about overexposure, even when I thought I was protected by the shade.

I now try to be more mindful about my UV exposure, even when I’m not using the Gem. I try to limit time spent outdoors during peak UV hours, and I try to get some sunlight on days spent mostly indoors. I credit the Gem with helping me better understand how my time outside could affect my well-being in ways I’d never considered, though the usefulness of your findings may vary.

At $249 outside of a deal, it’s expensive, though it’s nice that it doesn’t have a subscription. I personally can’t justify the price given how much time I spend indoors, but I see the appeal for people who spend considerable time outside, or those who want to be more mindful of sun protection. For the price, I wish the app’s guidance was clearer at times, and I wish it could track UV without the app being open in the background, but the Gem ultimately got me thinking less about how my body looks and more about how I’m treating it.

Southwest’s Friendly Airport Agents Can Cost You Money—Change Your Flight In The App Instead

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

Southwest’s free same-day flight change has a seat fee trap. Airport same day changes make separately purchased seats nonrefundable. Website, app and phone exchanges preserve that payment or trigger a refund, depending on the replacement seat’s price. You can be standing in the terminal, looking at the same replacement flight, but don’t talk to the agent! You’re better off with self-service. Those famously friendly Southwest agents come at a cost. For separately purchased seats: Change at the airport: the original seat purchase is nonrefundable. Website, app or telephone replacement seat costs the same or less: the payment carries over, without any difference refunded. Website, app or telephone more expensive replacement: pay the full new price. With limited exceptions, the old purchase is refunded to the original payment method, separately. You Need To Qualify For A Confirmed Change Free confirmed same-day changes require an available seat, the same origin and destination airports, and the same travel date. Make the change at least 10 minutes before your original scheduled departure. Choice, Choice Preferred and Choice Extra fares qualify, as do Getaways by Southwest vacation bookings. Basic economy customers have to upgrade their fare first even as A-List and A-List Preferred members. Partner itineraries aren’t eligible. Standby is different: you’re waiting for space on an earlier flight, rather than confirming the replacement immediately. Paid seats become nonrefundable when standby clears, including through the app. Elite members on Basic can qualify for standby even though they can’t make a free confirmed change on that fare. List at least 30 minutes before your original scheduled departure using the app or mobile web, or 10 minutes before it in person. A Credit For A Seat Can’t Buy Another Seat One September traveler reported paying about $230 for seats for a trip with their wife, then cancelling and rebooking after a medical emergency. They expected the seat credits to buy new seat upgrades, but the credits were only usable for airfare. Voluntarily cancel at least 10 minutes before original scheduled departure and the seat payment becomes airfare-only credit, for travel completed within 12 months of the original seat purchase. I’ve already covered Southwest displaying different prices to different travelers for the same seat. Customers now need to track the seat purchase separately from the ticket itself, including what happens when either changes. When Southwest downgrades you to a lower seat category, it promises to refund a separately purchased seat to the original payment method. When you exchange two paid connecting seats for a nonstop or direct (same-plane) flight, a replacement costing more than one or both originals triggers only one automatic refund. You need to request the second. Topics on this pageAirline ticketCustomer serviceSouthwest AirlinesMobile app+1 more More From View from the Wing

After Star Wars Zero Company dev Bit Reactor brought back an unspecified number of furloughed devs, some of those who weren't have seemingly been laid off

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

"I’m really proud of what we built together"

Early last month, Star Wars Zero Company developer Bit Reactor brought back a number of developers who had been furloughed before the game launched in August. At the time they did not specify how many devs were returning to the studio, with a separate report claiming more than half had become full time staffers again. Unfortunately, it does now seem like not everyone was able to be brought back, with a number of now former workers sharing word of having been laid off.

As spotted by Star Wars outlet Bespin Bulletin, multiple developers shared word of their layoffs to LinkedIn. A producer at Bit Reactor, Michael Blackard was seemingly one of the first to report being laid off, alongside senior QA analyst Ryan Mosher. Ryan Lopez, an environment artist at the studio, shared, "I was laid off from Bit Reactor this week after a three-month furlough, and while it stings, I’m really proud of what we built together." Lopez had apparently been working on the game for more than three years, starting all the way back on the game's pre-production period.

Tyler Eichten, a UI engineer, also said, "As of today, my time with Bit Reactor has come to a close due to a layoff. I am immensely proud of the work that I, the UI/UX team, and the team at large have accomplished. I hope that in this tumultuous industry that my coworkers (and myself) find placement." It's unclear at this point in time if any, or how many, other staffers have been formally laid off, and Bit Reactor are yet to make a comment.

Star Wars Zero Company performed very well both critically and commercially, making it all the more disappointing that not all of those who worked on the game, who made it as great as it seemingly is, get to continue working at the studio. As always, solidarity with all devs affected.

STAT+: BIO joins the legal fight against Medicare pilot that ties drug prices to peer countries

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

Test your knowledge with our new weekday mini crossword

John Wilkerson, a Washington correspondent, is the author of D.C. Diagnosis, a twice-weekly newsletter about the politics and policy of health and medicine.

John writes about the politics of health care, and he is the author of the twice-weekly D.C. Diagnosis newsletter. You can reach John on Signal at John_Wilkerson.07.

WASHINGTON — The Biotechnology Innovation Organization is joining the legal fight against the Trump administration’s pilot program aimed at aligning drug prices in Medicare with prices charged in peer countries.

BIO and two California-based biotech trade groups filed a complaint Friday in the U.S. District Court for the Northern District of California asserting that the Global Benchmark for Efficient Drug Pricing demonstration, known as GLOBE, oversteps Medicare’s statutory authority. The complaint seeks to halt the model.

The Centers for Medicare and Medicaid Services did not immediately respond to a request for comment.

Blackburn Rovers 2 – 1 West Ham United | Final whistle, first impressions as new manager bounce falls flat

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

West Ham managed to overturn their recent sequence of dreadful, lethargic starts with bright first half performance against Blackburn Rovers. Both sides had their chances but West Ham’s attack dominated the first half, largely through Austbø and Ezra Mayers down the left hand side.

The Hammers set up with Engels the deepest midfielder sitting in front of the back four and Kanté plus Mukasa the more forward of the trio. It seemed to protect the back line a little better as, apart from one looped shot over Hermansen’s goal, Blackburn’s threat was contained during the first 45 minutes.

Three or four times in the first ’45 Mayers overlapped after combining with Austbø – looking far more potent a threat than Solomon who he’d replaced – and got excellent crosses in from the left, an attacking option that had been lacking with Morato as the full back.

Piroe headed against the post early in the second half after Austbo – again – twisted and turned on the left, and Engles crashed the rebound just wide. Waves of pressure developed on the Blackburn goal as the Hammers responded to their half time talk but against the run of play from a break down the other end, Blackburn took the lead through Gudjohnson who headed through Hermansen’s legs. West Ham behind again.

Parker responded with Solomon for Austbø who’d run his socks off for an hour and Taty appearing in place of Kanté.

Controversy as Rovers scored a second with Kilman prostrate after a head collision – Dapo Afolayan the ex West Ham boy fired one into the corner leaving West Ham with a mountain to climb. It must be said, naive defending from West Ham.

Joel Piroe grabbed one back after a lucky bounce sat nicely for him in the box: 2 – 1 with eight minutes to go and the Ewood Park atmosphere changed, the home fans nervy and the Hammers fans in full voice. The minutes ticked away and West Ham struggled to get the ball towards Blackburn’s box: Orford, a late substitute, very fortunate to avoid conceding a penalty in the closing minutes – once again the lack of VAR coming to West Ham’s rescue.

Five minutes of added time, West Ham looking as if they had neither the urgency or the ability to surge forward again, fiddling about on the edge of their own box with Hermansen being booed every time he touched the ball. One last chance as Soucek headed straight at the ‘keeper in the last few seconds and that was that. Poor. Very poor. So much possession and so little to show for it.

Wardogs Season 3 brings an urban map, Limited Mode, and a 185,000-account ban wave

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

Wardogs' Season 3 will launch on 12th November, complete with a new urban map, a new Limited Mode, and a ban wave targeting 185,000 accounts.

Developer Bulkhead confirmed the details earlier this week, revealing plans for a full wipe and progression changes that it hopes will "recapture the shared discovery and sense of achievement that defined" the 100-player tactical shooter when it first launched in Early Access.

The new map will focus on close-quarters fighting, and for a "more level playing field", players can dip into Limited Mode, which offers "an alternative way to experience" the game as it restricts weapons, ammunition, armour, and vehicles.

While the full list of restrictions is still being decided, it currently includes FMJ-only ammunition, a Tier 2 armour cap, and a reduced attack vehicle selection.

"The mode is designed to preserve the feel of early-to-mid-game combat and reduce the advantage offered by more powerful equipment," the team said.

Bulkhead also confirmed seasons are getting longer. From now on, they’ll last three months, giving players "more time to work towards their goals before each wipe and the development team more time to prepare future updates". Progression will also be "brought closer to the pace of Season 1", following the studio’s acknowledgement that Season 2 accelerated "the journey too aggressively".

"Season 3 is about giving players a new battlefield to learn together and a progression journey worth spending time on," said Bulkhead CEO Joe Brammer. "Longer seasons give everyone more room to reach their own goals, and give us the time to properly develop what comes next."

Alongside Season 3, Bulkhead revealed it has used machine learning to review is "activating further layers of its anti-cheat" after analysing 187 million hours of gameplay with help from machine learning. It says having close to four million unique players since launch gave it enough data to pull that work forward from next year.

"[E]xploiters, real-money traders, and gold-farming networks" have been permanently banned and lost their server-hosting privileges, and a player trust score is planned for 2027.

"Protecting the people who play Wardogs fairly is essential to everything we're building," Brammer added.

Bigger changes to the metagame, including a new progression system, are also due in 2027 as Bulkhead works towards Wardogs 1.0. Full Season 3 patch notes will be out before launch.

Wardogs launched in Steam Early Access on 10th September, and is due on PS5 and Xbox Series X/S in 2028.

Wardogs is one of the breakout successes of the year. In our early access Wardogs review, we said the game's mix of competitive shooters' best bits with something entirely of its own made it "a brilliantly refreshing take on the genre".

GM Steps On Tesla’s EV Toes With EV1 Celebration

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

In a move that shocked and surprised nobody, last week Tesla CEO Elon Musk pulled the plug on his much-hyped Cybertruck. Production will end sometime next year. It may be small consolation, but at least the Cybertruck sold more units than another notorious flop in modern automotive history, General Motors’s ill-fated EV1 sedan. Was EV1 really a flop, though? In sales, yes, but GM wants everyone to know that the EV1 beat Tesla to the electrification punch in key technology milestones.

GM actually sold few, if any, EV1 units, preferring to lease the car with a focus on drivers in California. Production began in 1996 but that didn’t last long. In 2003 GM pulled the plug, recalled the vehicles, and sent them to the crusher, having produced barely more than 1,000 units overall.

Only a scant few escaped the junkyard. One of them was rescued by US filmmaker Alex Gibney, leading to his advisory role in the searing 2006 EV1 documentary “Who Killed the Electric Car?” (btw, Gibney ‘s newly released “Musk” documentary is available on streaming now).

This year marks the 30th anniversary of EV1 production, and GM is using the occasion to celebrate. Rather than shoveling all memory of the EV1 debacle into the memory hole, GM has embarked on sort of under-the-radar public relations campaign, seeking to position the car as a necessary, albeit short lived, step along the vehicle electrification journey.

In not so many words, GM also draws attention to the fact that its work on EV1 predates the launch of Tesla Motors, which came on the scene in 2003. The automaker’s Senior Writer and Editor at GM News, Chris Perkins, ran down the particulars in March, noting that EV1 was the first EV to deploy heat pumps for climate control, energy efficiency, and battery temperature management.

Perkins also takes note of the car’s regenerative braking system. “Engineers developed a system for the car that translated a driver’s brake-pedal input into an electronic signal that blended regenerative and friction braking,” Perkins wrote.

“It’s a precursor to the braking systems used in GM’s modern EVs, which also add One-Pedal Driving1 and paddle-actuated Regen-On-Demand2 capability,” he added.

EV1 also eschewed mechanical actuators in favor of fully electronic (aka by-wire) controls for the accelerator pedal, brake pedal, parking brake, and gear selector, with the power steering featuring electro-hydraulic control.

Additionally, Perkins elaborated on the regenerative braking angle in a follow-up article in August, by way of demonstrating a through line between the 1990’s technology and the automaker’s current EV lineup.

“Conceptually, the EV1’s braking system operates exactly like the braking systems in GM’s modern EVs,” Perkins wrote. “Press the brake pedal, and the car’s computers decide how much friction and regenerative braking to actuate. Should the electronic system fail, it’s connected to a mechanical backup, just as in the EV1.”

As part of the PR campaign, GM is also putting its 1994 concept sedan on display, the 1994 Impact (pictured above). “As one of the first modern, purpose-built electric vehicles from a major automaker, the Impact was a critical learning tool that shaped the final production version of the EV1,” GM emphasizes.

If you blink, you’ll miss it. The Impact is on display at the GM-sponsored Test Track at EPCOT, the Disney theme park in Bay Lake, Florida, until mid-December.

As for why all this fuss, why now, that’s a good question. Over the past several years, GM has put a jolly spin on its inability to catch up to Tesla in sales, let alone surpass it. As recently as last year, GM vigorously touted itself as #2 in EV sales in the US, second only to Tesla as a badge of honor.

Still, by August of 2025 the competition was beginning to take on a different tone, with GM pitching low-cost LFP (lithium-iron-phosphate) batteries as a near-term pathway to meeting, then beating Tesla.

The 2026 EV1 celebration elaborates upon the technology angle, underscoring GM’s claim on the pre-Tesla, 1990’s roots of innovation in the vehicle electrification field.

The new emphasis on technology also comes at a pivotal moment for EV sales in the US. The US market nosedived last fall after US President Trump and his Republican allies in Congress prematurely eliminated federal tax credits for EVs. However, activity continued to stir in some quarters, and Trump himself made the case for EVs more attractive when he decided to bomb Iran in February.

Nine months later, the price of gas remains stubbornly high, sparking renewed hopes that EV sales will rebound more quickly than first anticipated. In addition, a fresh round of innovation is on the horizon, with GM banking on its forthcoming LMR (lithium manganese-rich) battery formula to push costs down without sacrificing performance.

While Tesla is not the only competition in town, beating the startup at its own game would be a feather in GM’s cap, and Elon Musk made the effort a little easier when he canceled the Cybertruck, leaving Tesla without a foothold in the hot market for pickup trucks in the US. The automaker still has sedans, SUVs, the new Semi Class 8 truck, and the “flying” Roadster in its pocket, but the demise of the Cybertruck has left the pickup field wide open.

Meanwhile, Ford is on track to produce its new Fathom electric pickup next year starting at under $30,000, far less than the Cybertruck’s most recent starting price of $77,000 plus change. The Jeff Bezos-backed startup Slate Auto is also aiming at the affordability market with a stripped-down electric pickup starting at a base price of $26,000 and change, before accounting for add-ons.

On its part, GM is aiming for the middle ground with the 2027 Silverado electric pickup, starting at around $56,000 and reaching close to Cybertruck range at around $72,000.

Meanwhile, Rivian is also aiming upscale at close to $80,000 and up for its RIT pickup. The startup is on the move, having somehow managed to squeeze a loan guarantee of up to $4.5 billion out of the Trump administration. The funds are earmarked to expand Rivian’s production capacity with a new factory in Georgia.

Readers, what do you think? Does GM — or any other automaker — have a snowball’s chance in hell to catch up to Tesla in the US market? Drop a note in the discussion thread…

Farewell Roku aquarium: Popular screensaver will be removed following billion dollar Fox acquisition

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

On Nov. 6, Fox closed a $22 billion deal to acquire streaming device giant Roku, and the merger already has an unforeseen casualty: The end of the Aquatic Life screensaver.

Last week, fans of Aquatic Life — a relaxing aquarium filled with swimming fish — noticed an alert popping up on their Roku devices, warning that their fish would soon be gone. Because it was such a fan favorite, users took to social media to mourn the screensaver's end or plead for its future. Many wrote that Aquatic Life had become an essential part of their lives.

"Before my father passed I gifted him a Roku and one of the last conversations we had was him telling me he loved these fish so much," wrote one Reddit user.

"This screensaver has been a comfort to me for about ten years or more I can't remember. My wife picked it out and she loved it. Pancreatic cancer took her from me 7 years ago," wrote another.

Some theorized the popular, ad-free screensaver was losing the company potential revenue money. Roku's annual earnings reports over the last several years have positively cited the "high-visibility placements" of device advertising, including on its screensavers.

Others suggested Roku's new ownership may have been the nail in the coffin, with rumors circulating that the screensaver's developer had ended its licensing deal following Fox's entry. In an interview with ArsTechnica, Jim Sachs, the artist behind the screensaver, confirmed he chose not to renew his annual $80,000 licensing agreement after learning of the $22 billion deal. The deal was his primary form of income, Sachs told ArsTechnica. "I can’t be part of the Fox world," Sachs told the publication, alluding to the controversial reputation of Fox News. "It seems that Fox simply wants another venue for spreading their philosophy, which I consider repugnant. I can’t stand the idea of my artwork being used to lure even one person down that rabbit hole."

Users who currently have their screens set to the serene aquarium will be moved into Roku City on Dec. 1. Roku City — the company's default screensaver and native ad placement spot — has garnered its own cult following and meme status over the years. The company has leaned into the popularity heavily, adding viral easter eggs and pop culture references intended to stir online conversation.

But not everyone is a fan of the streaming metropolis.

"Likely because we live in a corporate dystopian hellscape and it's hard for them to shove advertisements into fish. This fucking sucks. No reason given. We just can't have nice things. Fuck Roku City," wrote one X user.

Roku has not publicly commented on customer demands to bring back Aquatic Life. Sachs said the company has asked him to renew the license, but that he wasn't willing to do so until Fox reverses its "rage-based business model" and chooses to "take responsibility for all the harm it has caused."

In the meantime, aquarium lovers can download the screensaver for Windows, macOS, iOS, and Android, or find an alternative on Roku.

Is Binance Whale Ratio Rising as BTC Falls?

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

Whether itâs Bitcoin or emerging altcoins, Yash breaks it down with clarity and precision.

Yash is a crypto analyst specializing in price analysis, predictions, and in-depth research reports. He combines technical indicators with on-chain data to uncover market trends and potential breakouts. His sharp insights help readers navigate the crypto market with confidence. Whether itâs Bitcoin or emerging altcoins, Yash breaks it down with clarity and precision.

Binance's whale ratio 100-day SMA reached 0.513 on November 6, its highest level since September 2017.

The Exchange Whale Ratio nearly touched 1.0 on October 31 as BTC fell from about $77,500 to $75,800.

The October 29 Binance hack may have affected large transfers, making the data harder to interpret.

Whales are taking a bigger share of Binance’s measured transaction flow, and the timing isn’t exactly reassuring. The Binance whale ratio’s 100-day simple moving average (SMA) reached 0.513 on November 6, its highest level since September 2017. Meanwhile, BTC slipped from roughly $77,500 on October 31 to $75,800.

The underlying Exchange Whale Ratio nearly reached 1.0 on October 31. That meant large-holder transactions accounted for almost all measured exchange flow that day.

The 100-day average then rose from 0.481 on October 31 to 0.513 on November 6. This increase came as BTC struggled, falling 11.3% over one month despite remaining 17% higher over three months.

There’s an obvious complication. Binance suffered a hot-wallet hack on October 29, with approximately $6.2 billion to $6.4 billion reportedly stolen.

That incident may have affected large transfers during the same period. So, treating the rising whale ratio as straightforward evidence of deliberate selling would be premature. The available figures don’t establish what those large transactions represented.

US job openings reached 7.52 million in September, according to JOLTS data released November 3. The November 6 NFP report was also scheduled during this period.

The dollar index stood at 101.56, roughly unchanged over one month. These macro indicators provide background, but they don’t directly measure Binance whale activity. Any connection remains speculative.

One possible explanation is that transfers linked to the hack or reactions to macroeconomic data lifted the whale share. Neither explanation is verified.

For now, the Binance whale ratio shows rising large-holder activity alongside a weaker BTC price. But the series still needs verification, and the short observation window doesn’t establish whether whales are selling, repositioning or simply moving funds.

Bitcoin ETFs flip negative for 2026 after $1.28 billion outflow week, worst since June

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

Get The Block’s latest crypto news and price alerts for the coins you follow, right in Chrome. Add our free extension here.

The 12 U.S.-listed spot bitcoin funds have now lost $914.1 million for the year, while ether, Solana and Hyperliquid ETFs all extended weekly outflow streaks in a brutal turn for crypto ETFs.

U.S. spot bitcoin ETFs logged $1.28 billion in net outflows last week, their worst week since late June, per The Block's analysis of SoSoValue data. It was the funds' second straight negative week and made the top ten largest weekly outflows since they launched in January 2024 at #7.

The funds saw outflows every day except Wednesday last week, per the data. They lost $398.6 million on Monday, $115.6 million on Tuesday, $430.2 million on Thursday and $411.9 million on Friday, with Wednesday's relatively modest $74.6 million inflow marking the only positive day.

The most recent worse week ended June 26, when the funds shed $1.79 billion in what The Block reported at the time was their second-worst week on record. Bitcoin ETFs lost $202.6 million the week before last, including a $643.3 million Friday outflow that was the largest single-day exit in four months after the record Binance hack.

BlackRock's IBIT accounted for $403 million of Friday's loss, or about 88% of the day's gross outflows. Bitwise's BITB and Ark & 21Shares' ARKB each lost about $20 million, and Grayscale's GBTC shed $11.1 million. Fidelity's FBTC took in $42.8 million on Friday, after leading Thursday's outflows with $199.9 million, as The Block reported.

The week's outflows erased bitcoin ETFs' gains for the year. The funds entered the week with about $368 million in 2026 net inflows, after turning positive for the year in late September. Monday's outflows pushed that figure below zero, and the funds closed Friday at about $914.1 million in net outflows year to date.

The total value of bitcoin held across the 12 funds dropped 2.3% to $95.9 billion, equal to about 6.3% of the cryptocurrency's market capitalization. IBIT held $60.3 billion in net assets against $65.1 billion in cumulative inflows.

The outflows came amid a crypto and equities selloff after Tuesday's midterm elections. Bitcoin fell 5.6% on Thursday alone, and analysts linked the slide to the Democratic sweep of Congress and doubts over AI spending, The Block reported Friday. Analysts say Texas's blue wave in particular could result in slowed development in the state replete with bitcoin mining facilities and data centers.

Spot ether ETFs lost $365.3 million last week, per SoSoValue data, their sixth straight week of net outflows. The run has drained about $2.02 billion from the funds since late September. Ether ETFs have twice posted eight-week losing streaks, most recently when they matched that record in early July.

Like the bitcoin funds, ether ETFs had only one positive trading day last week, also on Wednesday, when they gained $53 million. Monday's $139.8 million outflow was the week's largest, followed by $100 million on Thursday and $93.2 million on Friday. BlackRock's ETHA accounted for $90.8 million of Friday's total, about 96% of the gross losses, mirroring the firm's bitcoin fund.

The products are now down about $405.6 million for 2026. Net assets fell 2.9% over the week to $12.84 billion, against $11.92 billion in cumulative net inflows.

Spot Solana ETFs lost $26.3 million last week, per SoSoValue, nearly all of it on Friday. It was their fifth straight negative week, the longest streak since the funds launched in October 2025, and brought five-week outflows to $165.4 million. The streak began the week the funds ended a record 14-week inflow run.

Bitwise's BSOL led Friday's Solana outflows with $14.4 million, followed by Grayscale's GSOL at $5.4 million. The funds held $1.31 billion in net assets as of Friday, down from $1.40 billion a week earlier, though they remain about $676.2 million positive for the year.

The three publicly traded Hyperliquid ETFs shed $15.6 million, also extending their outflow streak to five weeks, the longest since they began trading in May. The funds have lost $58.4 million over that stretch, and their net assets fell about 17% on the week to $374.1 million. Friday's $8 million outflow was led by Bitwise's BHYP.

Bitcoin traded near $75,880 on Saturday morning, while ether changed hands around $2,244, SOL near $89.84 and HYPE around $77.79, according to The Block's price pages.

‘Godzilla Minus Zero’ Barrels Toward Historic $32M Box Office Launch With ‘Cat in the Hat’ at No. 2

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

The sequel to 2023's 'Godzilla Minus One' is making noise as 'Ramayana' and 'Wild Horse Nine' also debut.

Godzilla Minus Zero is king of the box office in its opening weekend as the sequel feature looks to dominate the frame that also sees the debuts of The Cat in the Hat, Ramayana, Wild Horse Nine and Club Kid.

Hitting 3,254 North American theaters from GKids and Toho, Godzilla Minus Zero conquered $14.5 million on Friday, including Thursday previews, as it heads toward an estimated $32 million for the weekend. This is being touted as the highest-grossing live-action foreign-language opening at the box office since 2004’s Hero launched with $17.8 million. (It’s behind the debut of last year’s animated Demon Slayer: Infinity Castle with $70.6 million, while 2004’s The Passion of the Christ and its massive $83.8 million opening is not included in the category as an American-produced project). Filmmaker Takashi Yamazaki‘s sequel to 2023’s Oscar-winning Godzilla Minus One earned an A CinemaScore from audiences and is performing far above the distributor’s projected opening in the mid-$20 millions. Related Stories Movies Danny McBride's 'G.I. Joe' Movie Lands 2028 Release From Paramount Movies 'Godzilla Minus Zero' Roars With $6.7M in Box Office Previews Ahead of Face-Off With 'Cat in the Hat'

Ryunosuke Kamiki, Minami Hamabe and Min Tanaka star in the new movie that carries a reported budget in the mid-teens. It centers on postwar Japan dealing with attacks from the three-headed Ghidorah and the possibility of Godzilla’s return. Godzilla Minus Zero holds an impressive 90 percent critics approval on Rotten Tomatoes, with The Hollywood Reporter chief film critic David Rooney calling it a “thrilling jolt of marauding monster action.”

The sequel is making an even bigger footprint than Godzilla Minus One, which opened in December 2023 with $11.4 million domestically and reached $57.1 million for the run, adding $56.9 million internationally. The film rode its massive breakout success to become Japan’s first feature to win the best visual effects Oscar.

Warner Bros.’ The Cat in the Hat grossed $6 million domestically from 4,003 North American locations on Friday, including Thursday previews, and it is set to pounce on around $18 million for its opening frame. The studio had projected that the $92 million-budgeted film, which earned an A CinemaScore, would open with $20 million and hopes for word of mouth to help it play through the Thanksgiving holiday and into December.

Directors Erica Rivinoja and Alessandro Carloni’s animated adaptation of Dr. Seuss’ classic 1957 children’s book centers on the titular feline being given a final chance to prove his worth by helping a pair of siblings. Bill Hader leads the voice cast that also includes Matt Berry, Quinta Brunson and Xochitl Gomez. The movie marks Warner Bros. Pictures Animation’s first title since Bill Damaschke took over as president and chief creative officer in 2023.

Critics have been a bit aloof on the film, as it holds a 45 percent Rotten Tomatoes score from reviewers, with THR critic Frank Scheck calling it “more taxing than enjoyable.” It’s certainly getting better notices than Universal’s 2003 live-action The Cat in the Hat starring Mike Myers, which holds an 11 percent score on Rotten Tomatoes. That movie opened to $38.3 million domestically en route to a North American cume of $101 million against a $109 million budget.

Sony Pictures releases Prime Focus Studios’ Ramayana: Rise of a Legend in 1,433 North American locations, with the film grossing $2.4 million Friday and headed to a sum of roughly $7.3 million for the weekend and $10.8 million for the four days. Boasting the widest domestic launch ever for an Indian movie, director Nitesh Tiwari’s religious epic focuses on the life of Prince Rama, with the two-film project’s budget reported at $420 million and beyond. It holds a 76 score from critics on Rotten Tomatoes, along with an 89 from audiences.

Also likely debuting in the top five is Angel Studios’ Drummer Boy, with directors Ben and Joel David Smallbone’s historical drama grossing $3.5 million Friday from 2,850 North American locations for a weekend opening around $7.9 million. Earning an A CinemaScore, the faith-based movie focuses on an unlikely bond during the American Revolution and stars Joel David Smallbone in the title role. On Rotten Tomatoes, it has a 50 critics score and a 93 from audiences.

Academy Awards hopeful Wild Horse Nine opened in 1,100 North American theaters with around $2.4 million Friday for an estimated frame of $5.5 million. John Malkovich, Sam Rockwell and Steve Buscemi lead writer-director Martin McDonagh’s comedic thriller about two CIA operatives who travel from Santiago, Chile, to Easter Island in 1973. The Searchlight Pictures release holds a 94 critics score on Rotten Tomatoes, with Rooney’s review describing it as “uniquely affecting.”

Looking to squeak into the top 10 is Burns & Co and Fathom Entertainment’s Jimmy, with the Jimmy Stewart biopic collecting $832,000 Friday from 1,470 North American theaters for a likely $2 million frame. Scheck’s review calls star KJ Apa “top-notch” but deems director Aaron Burns’ film “heartfelt to a fault.”

Another Oscar hopeful launched in platform release as A24 debuted Club Kid on six screens, with the Cannes standout grossing $175,398 on Friday. Making his feature directorial debut, Jordan Firstman also stars as a party promoter who learns he has a 10-year-old son. Set to expand into Thanksgiving, it boasts a sterling 100 score from reviewers on Rotten Tomatoes, with THR critic Richard Lawson heralding it as “winning and clever.”

Amazon MGM Studios releases I Play Rocky in seven locations, and it grossed around $160,000 on Friday. Anthony Ippolito stars as Sylvester Stallone in director Peter Farrelly’s making-of story for the 1976 classic Rocky. In his review, Rooney wrote that the movie — which holds an 88 Rotten Tomatoes score from critics — creates “a wave of warm nostalgia.”

Among holdovers, A24’s much-discussed Elizabeth Holmes documentary You Can See Everything looks to pull in around $8 million for the weekend as it competes with Ramayana and Drummer Boy for third place. The domestic cume for directors Nathan Fielder and Lance Oppenheim’s feature stands at $73.4 million through Friday.

Next weekend brings the Johnny Depp studio comeback vehicle Ebenezer, along with How to Rob a Bank and Paper Tiger.

How Long Does It Take To Finish The Legend Of Zelda: Ocarina of Time Remake?

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

The time has finally come to return to one of Nintendo's best games, The Legend of Zelda: Ocarina of Time, with a brand-new look and streamlined gameplay. Though players who have played the original will undoubtedly have an advantage over those who haven't, the remake also makes it much easier for new players to navigate the world.

With all that said, while new players won't spend quite as much time on the remake as they might have if they were playing the original for the first time, the game is still paced to ensure you enjoy the journey.

As mentioned, the time it takes you to beat the Ocarina of Time remake largely depends on whether you've played the original and whether you're planning to 100 percent the game. If you are playing Ocarina of Time for the first time, ever, then you should expect to spend around 22–25 hours on the game's main story.

If you've played the original Ocarina of Time, you likely already know where to go and what you're looking for, meaning you will spend less time wandering than new players. So, your completion time should be closer to 18–20 hours. The small range factors in the fact that returning players are also likely to explore a bit to see all the changes and enjoy the new graphics.

The Legend of Zelda: Ocarina of Time's Switch 2 remake had a young development team, and they were told to ignore the Zelda timeline.

Of course, whether you've played the game before or not, reaching 100 percent completion in Ocarina of Time will take some time. Most collectibles are in the same general area as the original, but a few things have been moved around enough that new players will also need to do some searching. Not to mention, unless you played the original recently, you've likely forgotten the original locations anyway.

With all that said, if you're looking to 100-percent finish Ocarina of Time, you should expect to spend closer to 30 hours on the game. You can save yourself some time by being as thorough as possible the first time you pass through a new area or dungeon, but either way, you should expect to add somewhere between 8–10 hours to your standard story completion time.

We finally got a chance to play The Legend of Zelda: Ocarina of Time remake and it's still one of the best games ever made.

‘It’s Insane’: Building Tech CEO Slams L.A.’s Return to Wood Framing After Deadly Wildfires

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

The country—maybe even the world—is at a crossroads in building construction due to climate change, and one CEO is disappointed about where things are going.

"Ninety-nine percent of this community is rebuilding with wood," Ken Calligar says of Los Angeles County, which still trying to rebuild after the devastating 2025 Palisades and Alatadena wildfires. "It's insane."

"Unfortunately, that means they have designed in the next disaster," the founder and CEO of building technology company RSG-3D tells Realtor.com. "Builders say 'If we have to design in resiliency into a house, then no one can afford the house.' That's just baloney."

A former Wall Street investment banker who acquired disaster-resilient technology manufacturer Insteel Panelmex in 2018, Calligar has spent the past decade trying to get people to build differently. (The difference between "resilient" and "resistant" is complex, but Calligar prefers the term "resilient.")

Renamed RSG 3-D, his company manufactures framing panels made of steel wire and expanded polystyrene wrapped in concrete skins that can be molded into any shape and are practically indestructible—impervious to hurricanes, earthquakes, and fires.

Unsurprisingly, Calligar's firm has been busy in Los Angeles since the fires—but not busy enough as far as he is concerned.

He points out that not only are most destroyed residential buildings in the area being rebuilt with flammable materials—but they are now larger and closer together, increasing the chances that a conflagration can spread from one home to the next.

"A six-year-old could have said this wasn't a good idea," he says. "These towns are so vulnerable, they have no idea. To me, it's so obvious what the solution is."

In 2011, Calligar was doing a deep dive into building technologies for a start-up he was advising. The start-up didn't last, but Calligar's intense interest in creating buildings that could survive disasters did.

"Since the 1980, we have had decades of increasing frequency of natural disasters and the costs have gone up dramatically," he says. "In the 1980s, there averaged about four natural disasters a year. In the 2020s, we’re averaging about 23."

Beyond fires, there is an increasing frequency of other types of natural disasters—hurricanes, floods, tornadoes, shoreline erosion, extreme heat and cold. According to the 2026 Realtor.com Housing and Climate Risk Report, 23.1% of U.S. homes are vulnerable to severe or extreme climate risk from wind, flood, or wildfire, culminating in $11.2 trillion in risk exposure.

Then there is the human compulsion to build in risk-prone zones. "We like our views, our mountains and our seashores, and the population has been building in more vulnerable areas," says Calligar.

Homeowners are already paying the price in higher HOA fees, rising insurance premiums, elevated mortgage delinquency rates, and even in the inability to find insurance coverage at all.

"When disaster strikes, shoppers retrench, but the effect is short-lived," says the report. "Following the January 2025 LA wildfires, buyer interest in high-risk homes dropped 10%, yet fully recovered within one month."

"Waterfront access, mountain views, and river retreats matter more than the risk that comes with them."

Another alarming report says that California could lose up to 75% of its beaches either seasonally or permanently by 2100. Highly developed areas face "especially severe losses."

Calligar agrees—not only that homeowners want what they want and what they want isn't always good—but that builders keep making the same builds that have proven ineffective and even downright dangerous.

He notes one destroyed home in L.A. that burned down again during its rebuild. Someone had broken into the construction site to steal copper wire and sparked a fire. According to Calligar, the home was "all wood framed."

"The U.S. residential world is the only technology that hasn’t changed in the last half century," he adds. "It's actually gone backwards. The wood is worse, the productivity of the workers is worse, the quality of the builds is worse."

"If you refuse to learn, you're going to get the lesson again."

Some people, however, have learned. Jonathan Scott of the Property Brothers led the rebuilding of fiancée actress Zooey Deschanel's childhood home in Pacific Palisades. The 100-year-old Spanish Revival house was rebuilt to look exactly as it had before the fires—only this time it's using RSG 3-D technology.

"When Zooey’s parents walk in, this will feel like the original home," the HGTV reality star told KTLA. "All of these things we’ve thought about to make sure this house never ever burns down again. They’ll never have to go through what they went through last January."

"He is not connected to us financially," Calligar stresses of Scott's plentiful touting of RSG-3D technology in the press. "He's just a very happy client."

Calligar, who is currently working on about a dozen Los Angeles building projects, has three times that number scheduled for next year. "The demand is off the charts," he says. "It's a question of how much we can handle. We're still a small company, we can’t be everywhere."

Nor are his projects limited to L.A. Partners are using the technology in six states and in 20 international countries.

He says the notion that it costs significantly more to build a resilient home are a myth—and that using RSG-3D is "cost competitive against inferior building systems." Plus, additional costs can usually be absorbed with savings in energy and operating costs, and lower insurance premiums.

"You pull out way ahead, we've done the math," he says.

Another bonus? Homes can be built faster using the technology, since the panels are easy to erect, and don't require heavy machinery. Those concerned about aesthetics need not be, says Calligar. The panels are architecturally flexible, and can be curved or domed for any shape.

"Because of the strength of the system, you can do a lot more architecturally than you can with wood," he says. "We've got a lot of cool architecture."

Those sold on the idea of a disaster-resilient house can get it done with the firm's "trained ecosystem" of builders, architects, and engineers.

He compares RSG-3D technology to vaccines, and how they extended the human lifecycle.

"What we're trying to do is eradicate the diseases that are harming everyone," he says. "And extend the [building] lifecycle by centuries, by selecting the right materials."

He points to the Egyptian pyramids, erected with practically indestructible limestone and granite, and still standing tall after 4,500 years.

"If they'd done that with wood, we wouldn’t even know about them, they'd be muck in the sand," he says. "It's a good lesson in doing things right."

Her work can be found in news outlets worldwide, including Yahoo, SF Gate, New York Post, Seattle-Post Intelligencer, Marie Claire, She Knows, Huffington Post, and New York Magazine. She has an M.A. in journalism from Columbia University. In her spare time, she writes psychological thrillers under a pen name. She lives in Brooklyn, and her cat foster Instagram account has over 4 million views.

Monkeys that lived through a deadly outbreak showed surprising caution around food

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

Japanese macaques from groups hit by a deadly outbreak were more likely to avoid food on feces, even if they had never tested positive.

In the first weeks of COVID-19, many households wiped down their groceries before putting them away.

Japanese macaques that lived through a deadly outbreak of their own also showed greater caution around food.

Macaques from groups where the virus had spread refused food placed on a soiled surface far more often than those from a group it never reached.

And the virus they had survived isn’t even known to spread through food.

Stephan Kaufhold and Federico Rossano of the University of California San Diego‘s Department of Cognitive Science ran the tests at a primate sanctuary in southern Texas.

The researchers tested whether monkeys would eat a grape placed on feces.

The experiment measured food avoidance, which does not necessarily indicate disgust.

The sanctuary’s Japanese macaques descend from a troop moved from Japan to Texas in 1972. Today they live in four separate fenced enclosures.

In January 2020, the sanctuary confirmed that a virus called simian retrovirus Type II was circulating in the Japanese macaques.

A long-tailed macaque that had retired from a research laboratory had carried it in without showing symptoms.

Late in 2019, the virus had likely changed enough to spread to the Japanese macaques.

Some infected monkeys died quickly of severe bleeding.

Others carried it with no symptoms, and some later lost weight and fell ill with other infections, much as people with AIDS do.

The virus passes through blood and saliva, mostly in bites and scratches during fights, and it has never been shown to spread through feces or food.

Three of the four groups went through the outbreak. The fourth, called Kyoto, didn’t.

Between October 2022 and March 2024, the team set two baited objects on the ground inside each enclosure and filmed what happened.

One was a plain piece of wood. The other was real macaque feces, collected from healthy animals and sealed under clear epoxy so it looked fresh but had no smell and couldn’t infect anyone.

A grape was placed on each object, and every monkey was free to take both.

Monkeys from the three outbreak groups ate the grape off the feces in 47% of 117 trials. Monkeys from Kyoto ate it in 79% of 80 trials.

So a monkey that had lived through the outbreak usually took the clean grape and left the other one lying there. A monkey from Kyoto usually ate both.

Within the outbreak groups, some monkeys had tested positive for the virus and some never had.

Kaufhold’s team compared the two, and the uninfected monkeys avoided the dirty grape about as much as the infected ones did. Both avoided it far more than the Kyoto monkeys.

In an interview with Earth.com, Kaufhold said that was the result he hadn’t expected.

“What surprised us was that animals within the exposed groups that had not been infected avoided the contaminated food just as much as those that had,” he said.

Kaufhold said the team doesn’t know why some monkeys escaped infection. Their immune systems may have resisted it, or they may have stayed out of the fights that spread it.

“Either way, the shift in behavior seems to follow having lived through the outbreak, for example witnessing others getting sick and dying, rather than being infected oneself,” he told Earth.com.

Only nine monkeys in the outbreak groups are confirmed uninfected, and the team treats that comparison as preliminary.

The outbreak groups showed greater caution around feces, even though the virus is not known to spread that way.

Kaufhold said the pattern “suggests a general increase in pathogen sensitivity rather than avoidance of the specific transmission route.”

In another experiment in the same paper, the team tested macaques from seven species, one at a time, with a sliding table that held the same two objects.

Among those tested, 14 were born at the sanctuary and raised by their mothers, while 15 were former pets raised by people.

The former pets had about twice the odds of taking food off the feces that the sanctuary-born monkeys did, even after years of living in monkey groups.

Kaufhold told Earth.com that one possibility is that the former pets “missed the chance to learn these behaviors from their mothers and other macaques early in life.”

Earlier studies found that young macaques watch their mothers and older groupmates forage, and that what they see others do shapes what they eat.

There is a cost, Kaufhold said. “Pathogen avoidance is always a trade-off.”

“Being too sensitive means leaving resources like food on the table, while being too indiscriminate increases exposure to pathogens and potentially disease.”

The outbreak monkeys were paying that cost in grapes.

The researchers can’t rule out that the four groups already differed before the outbreak, because nobody tested them then.

They also can’t yet say whether each monkey learned the caution on its own or picked it up from the group.

One precedent they cite is a troop of wild baboons in Kenya. A tuberculosis outbreak in the 1980s killed the troop’s most aggressive males, and the calmer culture that followed was passed on to males who joined years later.

Kaufhold said two questions come next: how long the heightened caution lasts, and whether monkeys that join these groups later pick it up.

“If newcomers picked up the increased caution, that would suggest social transmission, which could lead to a group-specific tradition,” he said.

Kaufhold said the macaque data “does not directly speak to how human behavior changed during and after COVID-19, but there are some interesting parallels.”

Studies of people found that disgust sensitivity rose during the pandemic, including toward things that had nothing to do with how the coronavirus spread.

The authors argue that human disgust was built on an older system for avoiding disease, one that changes with experience.

The team hasn’t yet measured whether that caution will still be there in the Texas enclosures a few years from now. They hope to go back and find out.

The full study was published in the journal Royal Society Open Science.

Check us out on EarthSnap, a free app brought to you by Eric Ralls and Earth.com.

Non-league footballer in stable condition after collapsing in warm-up and rushed to hospital, leading to match being abandoned

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

By BEN WILLCOCKS, ASSISTANT SPORTS EDITOR

Published: 11:35 EDT, 7 November 2026 | Updated: 16:31 EDT, 7 November 2026

Truro City's match against Folkestone Invicta was abandoned due to a medical emergency on Saturday after a footballer collapsed during the warm-up.

Yassine En-Neyah, 26, was rushed to hospital in an ambulance after collapsing while preparing with his team-mates on the pitch. It was later confirmed he was in a stable condition.

The former Nottingham Forest academy youngster had been named among the substitutes to face Folkestone in the National League South game in Kent.

A club statement on Saturday read: 'Truro City can confirm that this afternoon's match against Folkestone Invicta has been postponed.

'Yassine En-Neyah required medical assistance during the warm-up and was transferred to the William Harvey hospital in Ashford for further treatment.'

The club added a welcome on Saturday night, saying: 'He is stable, sitting up and chatting, but will remain in hospital overnight under observation.

Truro City's clash with Folkestone Invicta has been abandoned due to a medical emergency

Yassine En-Neyah - pictured before the game - was named on the bench to face Folkestone

Club Statement:Truro City can confirm that this afternoon's match against Folkestone Invicta has been postponed. Yassine En-Neyah required medical assistance during the warm-up and was transferred to the William Harvey hospital in Ashford for further treatment.We will… pic.twitter.com/IyiF2VIFMz

'The club would like to place on record its thanks to Truro City's medical staff, their counterparts at Folkestone Invicta, along with all club personnel from the home club.

'Both the National League and Football Association have also offered their help and support. There have also been many well wishes from Truro City fans and the wider football community too.'

Truro City's players had earlier gathered around the rear of the ambulance where En-Neyah was being treated to shield him from view.

The Moroccan-born former Republic of Ireland youth international began his career at Nottingham Forest, making his professional debut in January 2020 as a substitute during an FA Cup third-round clash against Chelsea.

After being released by Forest in 2021, the winger has played for multiple non-league sides including Shelbourne, Waterford and Maldon & Tipree.

Having briefly represented Truro in 2023, En-Neyah returned to the Tinners for a second spell in 2024, where he has remained since.

When he signed his new Truro contract last season, En-Neyah admitted he didn't think twice about the decision to stay.

'Truro's been my home for the last couple of seasons and it was a very easy decision,' he told BBC Radio Cornwall.

'I feel home so I just want to continue working hard and help the club achieve greater things.

'It wasn't a case of having to think about it, it was just a matter of getting it done and continuing to work hard and do well for the club and for myself.'

Good Riddance to the Cybertruck, the Chariot of the Dumbest Gods

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

In early 2024, I started seeing one particular Tesla Cybertruck driving around Brooklyn. It was the first one I’d seen — a matte black version with a decal on the side that said “To the Moon,” a common phrase associated with trading cryptocurrency. I remember seeing it pop up in Williamsburg, in Greenpoint, and scattered spots throughout Bedford Stuyvesant and Crown Heights. At the time, it was a novelty — the only evidence I had seen in my little New York bubble that anyone was willing to buy these things.

Evidently, though, people did buy Cybertrucks. Just not enough of them. All told, Tesla sold around 70,000 of the vehicles between their release in November 2023 and Friday, when CEO Elon Musk announced that the company would wind down production in favor of a new self-driving taxi, the Cybercab. When the Cybertruck launched, Musk predicted it would sell 250,000 units a year, one of many confident claims the tech billionaire has delivered that wildly overshot the mark. Very few of his products, it turns out, do actually go to the moon.

But how could we ask for a better emblem of this stupid, violent, get-rich-quick era of America? The Cybertruck was a vehicle designed to the specifications of one man, who made himself the richest person in the world through a savvy combination of government kickbacks, stock manipulation, and viral marketing stunts. They were designed to look like a futuristic war-vehicle, manufactured in Texas at an absurdly named building called the Gigafactory, and equipped with bullet-proof side panels and doors. The Cybertruck was a car built for the paranoid fantasies that have gripped so much of a country destabilized by generations of economic theft. Electric to assuage the existential worry of climate change, bullet-proof to match America’s extremist gun culture and pervasive fear of crime, shaped like a video-game toy to please the frontal lobes of a generation doused in the recycled franchises of comic book heroes and supersoldiers.   Editor’s picks The 250 Greatest Albums of the 21st Century So Far The 100 Best TV Episodes of All Time The 500 Greatest Albums of All Time 100 Best Movies of the 21st Century

They were also absolutely dogshit cars. The electric systems were prone to software bugs. Some trucks were known to shut down completely when they got wet. Their odd shape and bizarre design made them impractical for hauling, loading, or towing anything. In 2025 the company recalled 46,000 of them because it found they had a specific piece of side panel that fell off in the cold. Almost immediately after they came out, automotive YouTubers and professional mechanics posted videos of Cybertrucks that came in with ill-fitting body work and shoddy production quality, in itself a common hallmark of Tesla’s more recent vehicles. To be fair, the doors were bulletproof, provided you shot them with a small enough caliber of gun and didn’t aim for the windows.

Shortly after announcing the Cybertruck, Elon Musk made an overt political move in backing Donald Trump’s 2024 campaign for president, pouring nearly $300 million into a dark money organization called America PAC and other ventures. Online, he ranted about trans people (he has a trans daughter who hates him), immigrationillegal immigrants (he was born in apartheid South Africa), and “woke” politicians pushing DEI policies (his factories were reported to be alleged hotbeds of racism and sexual harassment that have resulted in multiple lawsuits). When Trump took office in 2025, Musk leapt into a role as head of the Department of Governmental Efficiency, or DOGE, which itself was a reference to a 2010s-era Reddit meme about the Shiba Inu dog breed that subsequently inspired one of the first crypto “shitcoins.” DOGE promptly fired thousands of federal workers, ground multiple government agencies to a halt, and eviscerated USAID, a decision which directly contributed to tens of thousands of preventable deaths overseas of disease and famine in resource-starved regions of the developing world.

Excited dorks who had signed up to buy the Cybertruck found themselves having to slap on bumper stickers that said “I bought this before Elon went crazy” so they wouldn’t get vandalized in public. Others, like the crypto nerds and Trump-friendly bros who made up the enormous middle section of the MAGA-Musk Venn Diagram, often decided to double down, wrapping their Cybertrucks with right-wing decals and tinting their windows with American flags. Simply driving one in public became a political statement, whether the driver intended it or not: I’d be lying if I said I didn’t sneer a little every time the matte-black to-the-moon-mobile drove past me in Brooklyn. Related Content The Midterms' Biggest Loser Is Elon Musk ‘The Money Comes With Baggage’: How Texas Dems Are Withstanding Elon’s Dark Money Deluge Elon Musk’s Grok Finally Delivers First Installment of ‘Historically Accurate’ ‘The Odyssey’ If You Take Elon Musk at His Word, Tesla Just Debuted a ‘Flying Car’

I’ll admit, I was predisposed to hate the thing. A decade ago, in what feels like another life, I was a tech reporter at a startup publication that catered specifically to the Cybertruck demographic (though the truck itself didn’t exist yet). Our audience loved Marvel movies, sci-fi nostalgia, and Obama-era techno-optimism. Above all else, they loved Elon Musk. Musk, at the time, was presenting himself as a future-focused benevolent tech pioneer, with cameo roles in Iron Man II and a non-stop stream of quirky gags online, like his cars’ “Ludicrous Speed” mode and an Easter egg on the onboard computers that played the “Nyan Cat” meme. If those words mean nothing to you, that’s probably a good thing. Trending Stories A Jesuit Priest Explains How He Facilitated Bob Dylan’s Meeting With the Pope Richard Lloyd, Television's Virtuoso Guitarist, Dead at 75 Houston Mayor Limits Cooperation With ICE: ‘Elections Matter’ Good Riddance to the Cybertruck, the Chariot of the Dumbest Gods

What it showed, however, was Musk’s core nature, as a nihilistic salesman who would say anything in order to sell his products, no matter how divested from reality they were. Case in point, in 2017 Musk said that the “Fully Self Driving” feature on his Tesla vehicles could make them capable of “hands-free cross-country road trips” by the end of the year. Autonomous driving technology has come a long way since then, but the road trips never happened. Instead, Musk moved on to other schemes and startups, some successful, some not. The Cybertruck’s successor, the Cybercab, hopes to compete with Google’s driverless Waymo project, though neither vehicle is capable of those fully autonomous road trips yet. Maybe in another 10 years.

By that time, I would be shocked if there are any Cybertrucks left on the road. Perhaps they’ll sit on blocks in the front yards of high-school dropouts who hit it big in the 2020s crypto booms. More likely they’ll end up as scrap, their somewhat bullet-resistant steel panels crumpled down or gathering rust while their massive lithium-ion batteries leech chemicals into the groundwater of some unfortunate industrial dump-adjacent community. Elon Musk, of course will still be rich, his companies subsisting off of an endless spigot of government cash and circular stock buybacks, no matter how many elections the Democrats manage to win. But maybe, if you squint a little, you can see a different future, one in which the occasional real and valuable advances made under mercurial hucksters are used for the greater good, instead of hoarded by monopolies enabled by politicians on the take. Maybe someday we’ll see the Cybertruck as a relic of a dark period in our past, and not a dire portent of the even more broken future down the road.

On this day in space! Nov. 7, 1992: NASA launches Mars Global Surveyor, its 1st Mars mission in 20 years at the time, to map the Red Planet like never before

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

Mars Global Surveyor looked at the south pole of Mars and saw signs of possible climate change.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

On Nov. 7, 1996, NASA launched its Mars Global Surveyor mission. This was the first mission NASA sent to Mars in almost 20 years!

The Mars Global Surveyor spacecraft was an orbiter. It transmitted data from NASA's Mars rovers Sojourner, Spirit and Opportunity back to Earth. The spacecraft also did a lot of its own science. It found surface channels on Mars that appeared to have been created by running water.

Mars Global Surveyor looked at the south pole of Mars and saw signs of possible climate change. It also saw a mineral called hematite on Mars. Hematite usually forms in water. NASA then sent its Spirit and Opportunity rovers to look for the hematite, and both rovers found it near their landing sites.

Mars Global Surveyor survived much longer than expected. But a space maneuver accidentally exposed one of its batteries to the sun, and the spacecraft lost power. It fell silent forever in November 2006.

As the first successful Mars mission since the two Viking launches in 1975, Mars Global Surveyor ushered in a new era of exploration of the Red Planet, not only through its own contributions to orbit-based science, but by serving a relay for surface-based rovers. As such, it became a primary pipeline for multiple concurrent Mars missions."The [Mars Global Surveyor] mission overhauled scientists' understanding of Mars by studying the entire Martian surface, atmosphere and interior. Major findings included dramatic evidence that water still flows on Mars in short bursts down hillside gullies, and the identification of water-related mineral deposits leading to selection of a Mars rover landing site for a subsequent mission," read a NASA description of the probe.

Mars Global Surveyor sent back more than 240,000 images to Earth during its nine active years.Want more space history? Check out our full On This Day In Space Story archive and watch our On This Day In Space videos on YouTube.

On this day in space! Nov. 6, 1572: A German astronomer discovers the Tycho supernova with the naked eye, but everyone thought the cosmic explosion was a 'new star'

Hanneke Weitering is a multimedia journalist in the Pacific Northwest reporting on the future of aviation at FutureFlight.aero and Aviation International News and was previously the Editor for Spaceflight and Astronomy news here at Space.com. As an editor with over 10 years of experience in science journalism she has previously written for Scholastic Classroom Magazines, MedPage Today and The Joint Institute for Computational Sciences at Oak Ridge National Laboratory. After studying physics at the University of Tennessee in her hometown of Knoxville, she earned her graduate degree in Science, Health and Environmental Reporting (SHERP) from New York University. Hanneke joined the Space.com team in 2016 as a staff writer and producer, covering topics including spaceflight and astronomy. She currently lives in Seattle, home of the Space Needle, with her cat and two snakes. In her spare time, Hanneke enjoys exploring the Rocky Mountains, basking in nature and looking for dark skies to gaze at the cosmos.

AC Milan Women 0-3 Inter: Chances squandered and a silly red card

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

Milan Women failed to replicate the heroics of the men’s team in the Derby della Madonnina, conceding three goals against Inter after a silly red card.

After the odd-goal defeat to Como last night time out, Milan Women were out for revenge this afternoon, and perhaps also a little inspired by the men’s win in the derby last weekend. However, Suzanne Bakker’s side made life very difficult for themselves.

Read SempreMilan ad-free and get access to exclusive news. Click here for a free trial!

In the first half, Milan actually had a couple of good opportunities to take the lead and make it a very different game. After just four minutes, the veteran Christy Grimshaw got a good chance in the box, but failed to connect with the cross from the left with her head from close range.

About ten minutes later, Mawa Sesay picked up on a loose ball after a mistake by Inter’s backline. However, she somehow missed the target and didn’t even force the goalkeeper to a save, effectively squandering a 1v1 by rushing the shot.

In the second half, the game remained fairly even but one moment proved costly for Milan. As Benedetta Glionna made her way into the box, the aforementioned Grimshaw made her evening worse with a little kick, resulting in a penalty for the away side (somewhat cheap, but fair).

Haley Bugeja made no mistakes from the spot, firing a great shot straight down the middle to give her side the lead in the 57th minute. Milan then began their pursuit of an equaliser after that but instead of a goal, around five minutes later, Sandra Ogalla was sent off.

The goalkeeper used her hands a little bit too early, touching the ball outside the penalty box, and the referee immediately spotted it. Bakker made use of the VAR challenge, prompting the referee to have a look at the screen, but the decision was upheld. A silly red card indeed.

Deputy goalkeeper Sally Rainey was brought on but just a few minutes later, Inter doubled their lead through Glionna. It was a well-worked move, in fairness, but the defending could have been better at the far post. That was in the 67th minute.

Milan tried to get back into the game but struggled with only ten players. And so, in the 83rd minute, Inter were awarded another penalty. This one was even cheaper than the first, as Angelica Soffia barely touched the player.

Despite another VAR check, the decision was upheld and Bugeja bagged her second of the night. That concluded the night from a goalscoring point of view, and Milan Women suffered their second consecutive defeat.
