Richard Lloyd, Television’s Virtuoso Guitarist, Dead at 75

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

Richard Lloyd, the Television co-founder and guitarist who helped bring a deft virtuosity to punk’s stripped-down fury, has died at the age of 75.

Lloyd’s family said in a statement that the guitarist died Saturday morning at his home in Chattanooga, Tennessee. “His loving wife Sheila and his dog Willie Mays were with him at the end,” his family said in a statement to Rolling Stone, adding that Lloyd died from Cholangiocarcinoma, a bile duct cancer.

Lloyd was diagnosed with bile duct cancer in March 2026. According to a GoFundMe set up to help Lloyd cover medical expenses and other costs, the guitarist developed tumors in his liver, including one that was so close to a major blood vessels, his doctors were unable to operate and remove it.

Television were one of the most innovative bands of punk’s first wave, thanks largely to the twin guitar attack of Lloyd and frontman Tom Verlaine. While many of their peers perfected a loud, fast pummel of power chords, Lloyd and Verlaine (who died in 2023) developed an intricate interplay of riffs and licks, and never shied from spiraling into headier realms inspired by jazz, art-rock, and even jam bands.

Lloyd and Verlaine’s stellar guitar work anchored Television’s seminal debut, Marquee Moon, which was released in 1977. That album cemented Television’s place in the punk pantheon, even though their output otherwise consisted of just two more albums, 1978’s Adventure and 1992’s Television.

Lloyd was born Oct. 25, 1951 in Pittsburgh, Pennsylvania and raised largely in New York City. His first instrument was the drums, but he was fascinated by the guitar and decided to switch after a “hallucinatory experience.” “My drums lost all their color — like in the Wizard of Oz in Kansas — and everything became grayscale,” Lloyd wrote in his 2017 memoir, Everything Is Combustible. “The drums didn’t sound good anymore, and I heard a voice — an auditory hallucination that told me, ‘You need to play a melody instrument.’”  Editor’s picks The 250 Greatest Albums of the 21st Century So Far The 100 Best TV Episodes of All Time The 500 Greatest Albums of All Time 100 Best Movies of the 21st Century

One of Lloyd’s first guitar partners was Velvert Turner, a musician from Brooklyn, who himself was developing his guitar skills under the tutelage of Jimi Hendrix. After dropping out of high school, Lloyd moved to Boston, where he played with blues legend John Lee Hooker. He also spent some time traveling and living in Los Angeles, but eventually moved back to New York City and embedded himself in the proto-punk scene there.

Television formed not long after Lloyd returned to NYC in 1973. His friend, the manager Terry Ork, took him to see Verlaine play at a club, and Lloyd knew instantly they would make a great musical pair. As he put it to The Stranger in 2015, “I saw Tom play and I thought, ‘He’s got it.’ And I knew I had it. But I was missing something and so was he. What he was missing, I could supply. And vice versa.”

Explaining what he had that Verlaine didn’t, Lloyd continued, “The fleshing out of all the songs. All the filigrees and arabesques on Marquee Moon are all mine. Even though the solos are maybe different, during the verses, when Tom’s singing, I’m playing all the melody. I brought a rock & roll heart.”

Joining Lloyd and Verlaine in the first iteration of Television was Richard Hell on bass and Billy Fica on drums. Hell eventually left and was replaced by Fred Smith, who’d been playing with Blondie. The band soon became a fixture at legendary punk clubs like CBGB and Max’s Kansas City. They released a debut single, “Little Johnny Jewel,” in 1975, before signing with Elektra Records, which released Marquee Moon two years later.

One of Lloyd’s most impressive contributions to Marquee Moon was his idea to double track some of his most intricate guitar parts. As he wrote in Everything Is Combustible, he thought it was “sad and pathetic” to go into a recording studio and come out with a record that just sounded like a band playing live in a room. Wanting to give the record something extra, he insisted to Verlaine and co-producer Andy Johns that he could replicate the part he’d laid down for “Venus” note-for-note to give the song more depth.  Related Content Television Guitarist Richard Lloyd Diagnosed With Bile-Duct Cancer Watch the First Teaser for ‘Monster: The Lizzie Borden Story’ Melissa Gilbert Urges Stage Parents to ‘Be Sure’ It’s What Child Wants in Wake of Daveigh Chase Death Tom Verlaine Had a Wildly Expansive Record Collection. Now, It's Going Up for Sale

“When I came back into the room they were saying they couldn’t believe it, but it sounded perfect,” Lloyd wrote. “Tom wanted me to do it on everything, but I knew that it was a kind of ear candy that should come in and out of the various songs to make it a real record. So, I refused to do it in some instances, and in others I did double or more.”

For example, on “Guiding Light,” he laid down eight different guitar tracks “which is why it sounds powerful and thick. I did my very best to make it a record — not a live document.”

Despite having the backing of a major label like Elektra, Marquee Moon failed to make a commercial dent, and the band’s opening tour slot for Peter Gabriel scarcely created more fans. Not long after the release of Adventure in April 1978, the band broke up. The following year, Lloyd released a solo album, Alchemy, which earned some positive reviews, but also went nowhere commercially.

During this time, Lloyd’s drug use was spiraling out of control. In his memoir, the guitarist admitted to overdosing on numerous occasions, and compared his experiences in the early Eighties to the Great Depression. “I could hardly draw people to gigs and I was thoroughly addicted to heroin,” he wrote. It wasn’t until 1984 that Lloyd got sober, and two years later he released a second solo album, Field of Fire.

Lloyd would release seven more albums over the course of his career, including a live record, 1987’s Real Time; a collection of unreleased material, 2010’s Lodestones; and what would be his final solo album, 2018’s The Countdown. He also toured regularly, worked as a session musician — including gigs with Matthew Sweet and X’s John Doe — and gave guitar lessons.

While Lloyd was part of Television’s reunion in 1992 and rejoined the band again for a stint in the early 2000s, he left ahead of a 2007 tour commemorating the 30th anniversary of Marquee Moon. In that interview with The Stranger, Lloyd expressed frustration that the band wasn’t focused on doing new things; he said that, after the release of their 1992 self-titled album, the band wrote and demoed eight songs, but nothing would likely come of them because Verlaine had “run out of lyrics.”  Trending Stories A Jesuit Priest Explains How He Facilitated Bob Dylan’s Meeting With the Pope Richard Lloyd, Television's Virtuoso Guitarist, Dead at 75 Houston Mayor Limits Cooperation With ICE: ‘Elections Matter’ Good Riddance to the Cybertruck, the Chariot of the Dumbest Gods

Lloyd evinced a complicated relationship with Verlaine during his latter years. On the one hand, he would insist he loved Verlaine and wouldn’t have spent 35 years in a band with him if he didn’t, but also characterized him as difficult to work. Lloyd also remained upset that Verlaine took all of Television’s publishing, even though Lloyd said he contributed significantly to the band’s songwriting.

Though unafraid to voice these opinions, Lloyd insisted he wasn’t bitter. As he told The Stranger, “I didn’t get into this for the money. Some people did. I got into it to become a renowned guitarist who’s made an irrevocable impact on rock & roll history.”

Chinas moon plan mirrors NASAs, except for one big difference

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

China's plan to land people on the moon by 2030 mirrors NASA's on paper but skips crewed lunar test flights, trusting robotic rehearsals for its first human landing instead.

Chinese space officials laid out their approach at the 7th Manned Space Symposium in Beijing on Oct. 31, according to Xinhua, the Chinese government's wire service. A slide reportedly from the presentation posted by CNSpaceflight, an independent outlet that tracks the Chinese space program, shows two of the initial missions with nobody aboard. A crew flies the Mengzhou, or "Dream Vessel," capsule to Earth orbit in 2027. Then the robots take over. The Lanyue, or "Embracing the Moon," lander touches down on its own in 2028, and an uncrewed mission rehearses the full landing sequence in 2029.

"For any crewed mission, we will try to fly it in an unmanned state first," said Wang Xiang, chief designer of the Chinese lunar exploration program, in the English captions of a CCTV report, China's state-run broadcaster.

The split cuts to the heart of a heated new space race and reveals two different institutional cultures. China's tight-lipped, military-run program trusts machines to prove its lunar hardware works. NASA's civilian program, working in public view to land astronauts first in 2028, leverages human test pilots. U.S. leaders say they're reviving the Apollo playbook that beat the Soviet Union to the moon: put crews on key test flights, launch often, and build on each mission.

"It's not entrepreneurial thinking or tech industry pixie dust or some new management philosophy," said NASA Administrator Jared Isaacman at a news conference on Oct. 23. "It's a playbook this agency knows really well — the same playbook that went from Apollo 7 splashdown to Apollo 11 in just nine months."

NASA has already flown one of those crewed test flights. Artemis II sent four astronauts around the moon in April. Next year, the four-person Artemis III crew will spend about two weeks in Earth orbit practicing maneuvers to dock the Orion spaceship with test versions of two commercial landers.

One lander is from Blue Origin, and the other is from SpaceX. Astronauts will climb into Blue Origin's lander to check its air and life-support systems, but will only briefly dock with Starship.

Earlier this year, NASA decided to do that test as a separate mission, shoehorned between Artemis II and the first crewed moon landing. If something goes wrong, "we can come home much more quickly," explained Jeremy Parsons, who manages NASA's Artemis program.

Apollo followed roughly the same script. Four crewed test flights came before Neil Armstrong and Buzz Aldrin landed in July 1969. China's first crew to head for the moon will be the one that lands.

Despite this major difference between the plans, NASA will lean on some robotics, too. SpaceX aims to land an uncrewed Starship on the moon by the end of 2027, and Blue Origin is working on its own uncrewed demonstration after Artemis III. NASA will keep both landers in the running as long as possible before picking one for its first crew, while China is building a single lander.

In other ways, the two plans look alike. Two Long March-10 rockets will launch China's capsule and lander separately, and the pair will link up in orbit around the moon. Two of the three crew members will then ride the lander to the surface and back. NASA's Orion carries four astronauts but also sends just two down. Blue Origin's lander even follows China's route, flying to lunar orbit on its own to meet the crew. SpaceX breaks the pattern, docking its Starship with Orion near Earth and pushing the spaceship to the moon.

Want more science and tech news delivered straight to your inbox? Sign up for Mashable's Top Stories newsletter today.

China says its uncrewed flights will make the first crewed landing safer. Its robotic lander also will gather some scientific data, measuring how hot the lunar surface gets and how the dust behaves. Its 2029 rehearsal will run every step, from docking in lunar orbit to landing, lifting off and docking again. The program has already completed early tests of its rocket, capsule, and lander.

The race, Isaacman warned, "will be much closer than it ever needed to be," emphasizing that China has the spacecraft, landers, and a "credible two-launch architecture" to put taikonauts, the Chinese term for astronauts, on the moon within three years.

NASA's own deadlines have slipped repeatedly. Artemis first aimed for a 2024 landing, and Isaacman recently acknowledged that Artemis III was running about three months behind schedule.

He conceded that urgency has its limits: "No amount of my enthusiasm or schedule pressure changes the laws of physics."

Elisha Sauers writes about space for Mashable, taking deep dives into NASA's moon and Mars missions, chatting up astronauts and history-making discoverers, and jetting above the clouds. YouTuber JinsTravel’s final video released after fatal Bolivia hit-and-run as driver gets house arrest

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

Travel YouTuber JinsTravel’s final pre-recorded video has been released days after he died when a truck struck him while cycling through El Alto, Bolivia. The driver involved has since been placed under house arrest while authorities investigate the fatal collision.

His latest journey began in Mexico before taking him through Peru and into Bolivia. Cho was riding through El Alto on November 4 when he was struck by a large cargo truck. Witnesses said the driver initially fled, but he was later apprehended.

Cho’s older brother announced that the family discovered a completed video while checking the late YouTuber’s official account. It had been scheduled to upload at 6:15 p.m. Korea Standard Time on November 7.

The family described the video as a final gift to JinsTravel’s viewers. His brother wrote that he hoped fans would “share in the final gift he left behind” and remember Cho for the journeys he documented.

The video, titled “A Healing-Filled Bicycle Journey into Bolivia, Made Possible by Chance,” follows Cho as he travels through the country by bicycle. Fans reportedly flooded the upload with messages of grief after realizing it would be his final journey published on the channel.

Cho’s family has been traveling to Bolivia with assistance from the South Korean Embassy to arrange the repatriation of his remains. They have also asked viewers not to spread unverified speculation or footage related to the crash.

According to El Diario, a judge ordered the 54-year-old truck driver to remain under house arrest following a pretrial hearing.

The crash happened on Avenida 6 de Marzo near El Alto’s Zona Franca. Local reports say the driver attempted to leave the scene before other transport workers pursued and stopped the truck, later handing him over to police.

The case remains under investigation as authorities work to establish exactly how the collision happened. Earlier reports said the driver claimed he did not see Cho’s bicycle, while police confirmed that a breathalyzer test found no alcohol in his system.

Wall Street can stop a tokenized share, but can it stop a stock perp?

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

Wall Street is fighting over crypto-style stock trading as major companies reject tokenized shares and traditional exchanges challenge new derivatives markets.

Costco recently made news when it strongly objected to a blockchain version of its shares. And while the supermarket giant did raise a set of pretty valid concerns regarding the potential risks of tokenization, the problem was that traders can actually already buy a blockchain-based contract (i.e., token) that follows its stock price.

The company is among 67 issuers in OKXICE's Nov. 5 objection notice, blocking third-party tokenized versions of their stock from the proposed venue backed by the New York Stock Exchange's owner.

Meanwhile, Kalshi lists a Costco perpetual, which offers price exposure without giving its buyer any shares.

The two products show how differently finance treats owning a company and trading its price: an issuer can stop its stock tokens from trading through one blockchain arrangement, while contracts tied to that stock follow a separate legal route.

That route is now being challenged in court, with established Wall Street firms competing to offer these contracts.

Shares give shareholders an interest in a business, along with the voting and distribution rights attached to the particular class of stock they own. Putting that interest on a blockchain can change how it's transferred while preserving what its owner is entitled to receive.

OKXICE's proposed model uses a tokenization provider acting through a registered broker-dealer to hold one underlying share for each token. Each token represents an entitlement to that share, and customers would trade them against dollar-linked stablecoins in automated asset pools.

Those pools can produce different prices from conventional exchanges. The ownership claim and the price you can get for it are separate concerns.

The Securities and Exchange Commission's September exemption allows qualifying venues to operate outside ordinary exchange registration, subject to conditions.

Tokens must preserve equivalent shareholder rights, including votes, dividends, and a claim on residual assets if the company is liquidated. Any token that just copies a share price doesn't qualify.

But the underlying company also gets a say. Before offering stock tokenized by an unaffiliated third party, an exchange has to notify the issuer, and each company has 30 days from receiving its notice to object.

The SEC's staff guidance confirms that a timely objection must be disclosed and prevents the venue from offering that token under the exemption.

Costco and dozens of others have already exercised that right, with their objections applying to OKXICE, and the companies haven't collectively explained their motives. The list therefore tells us little about their views on other forms of tokenization or contracts referencing their share prices.

A Costco perp works differently. You go long to gain when the stock rises, or short to gain when it falls. The contract has no scheduled expiry, and Kalshi's stock-perps documentation says holders receive neither shares nor voting rights.

Reported trailing 24-hour notional volume from one Kalshi data snapshot retrieved Nov. 6, 2026, at 13:49 UTC, rounded to the nearest $1,000. Covers only the four stocks named in Kalshi's Nov. 3 launch announcement, not its full stock-perp market. The exact rolling-window cutoff is not separately timestamped. Turnover is not investor count, new capital, or a measure of bullish demand.

Source: Kalshi market data, retrieved Nov. 6, 2026, 13:49 UTC · Kalshi's Nov. 3 stock-perpetual launch announcement

That also means you can take substantial exposure with relatively little money. Costco's market page showed maximum leverage of 4.9 times on Nov. 6, meaning $1,000 of collateral supports $4,900 of price exposure.

A 10% move would cost $490 before fees and other adjustments, nearly half the original collateral. If collateral falls below the required minimum, the platform can close the position.

Even though the contract has no expiry, you may lose the ability to wait for a recovery.

Thanks for signing up to The Catalyst. Look out for your first daily email. If you already receive it, you’re all set.

Assumes identical percentage price moves and unchanged position size. Loss = starting exposure x price decline. Excludes fees, funding, dividends, lot-size rounding and other adjustments. Liquidation could occur before an illustrated decline, so the bars are not a path an actual position can necessarily survive. The 4.9x input is Costco's displayed maximum on Nov. 6, 2026.

Source: Kalshi Costco market: displayed maximum leverage on Nov. 6, 2026 · Kalshi stock-perp mechanics and liquidation risks; illustrative calculations by CryptoSlate

Perps also need a way to keep their price near the underlying asset. Conventional futures have an eventual settlement date, while perpetuals use recurring payments between traders, called funding, to discourage a persistent gap.

When the contract trades too high relative to its reference price, longs have to pay shorts, and when it trades too low, payments run the other way.

These funding transfers can reward taking the less popular side, but they also mean that correctly predicting a stock's direction doesn't fully determine your return.

Kalshi says cash dividends generate contractual cash adjustments for long and short positions, although those payments don't make contract holders shareholders, nor does perpetual mean permanently open.

Kalshi's Costco page lists trading from Sunday evening to Friday afternoon, Eastern time. Its documentation warns that customers cannot open or close positions during closures.

On Nov. 3, CFTC Chairman Michael Selig said equity perpetuals could begin trading on eligible exchanges registered with the derivatives regulator and notice-registered with the SEC. He described the products as deemed approved under Regulation 40.3, which permits approval when the review period expires without a notice of non-approval.

The agency treats them as security futures, placing them under a joint regulatory framework. Their lack of an expiry date is at the heart of the challenge to that classification.

The Capital Markets Alliance Association, whose court filings include declarations from Cboe and Citadel Securities, wants to stop the approvals. It argues that a contract without future delivery cannot qualify as a security future.

In an October SEC submission, Cboe argued that the contracts fall under security-based swaps, agreements that transfer financial exposure linked to securities under different rules. It also warned that stock-perp trades would escape the securities markets' consolidated surveillance system even when traders hedge them with ordinary shares.

Cboe has a commercial stake in the outcome, but its argument raises a substantive issue about which safeguards should follow a trade that tracks a stock without delivering it.

Judge Valerie Caproni's Nov. 3 order found the challengers likely to succeed in arguing that the CFTC exceeded its authority. She nevertheless denied an emergency restraining order because the alliance hadn't demonstrated the imminent, irreparable harm required for that relief.

The approvals remained undisturbed at that stage, despite the judge's preliminary assessment of the CFTC's legal position. The order set Nov. 6 for the government's opposition to a preliminary injunction and Nov. 10 for the alliance's reply.

Meanwhile, the NYSE's parent is pursuing both markets. ICE announced an investment and agreement with Trade[XYZ] on Oct. 29 to bring its Hyperliquid-based perpetual markets into the US.

The planned regulated venue still requires approvals, including for leveraged trading. ICE expects distribution to include its OKX joint venture, subject to that venture's own approvals.

The same exchange group backing tokenized shares sees an opportunity in selling exposure to their prices. The dispute is partly over which institutions can offer crypto's trading model, and which rules they must follow.

The difference for investors is what each product promises. Qualifying share tokens must preserve shareholder rights while introducing the costs and risks of their trading platform. Perps offer leveraged exposure governed by collateral requirements, funding payments, and contractual adjustments.

Costco's objection shows how an issuer can stop the first product on a particular venue, while the court challenge concerns regulators' authority to approve the second. The ticker may be familiar, but the rights and risks come from the contract you buy.

Valve hides Dead by Daylight review bomb after TheBurntPeanut fallout

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

Valve has excluded a week of Dead by Daylight reviews from the game’s Steam score after it was review bombed over the TheBurntPeanut controversy.

Reviews posted between November 1 and November 6 are now marked as off-topic on the game’s Steam page, pushing its recent rating back up to Mostly Positive after it dropped to Mixed.

The page now shows an asterisk next to the game’s score, noting that Valve had detected an unusual amount of review activity during that period unrelated to the game itself.

Steam has corrected the Dead by Daylight review score back to "Mostly Positive" following the review bomb after TheBurntPeanut dramaThousands of reviews were negated due to their nature being "largely unrelated" to the game pic.twitter.com/bifbJCVKX2

Thousands of reviews were wiped from the score. Negative reviews during that week came from both sides of the controversy, with some players criticizing Behaviour for working with TheBurntPeanut and others coming from the streamer’s fans.

Valve has used its off-topic review system since 2019. When it detects a spike in reviews unrelated to the game, it flags that window and leaves those reviews out of the score by default. Players can still choose to include them manually.

At the time of writing, Dead by Daylight sits at around 78% positive overall from almost 950,000 reviews, according to SteamDB.

The reviews poured in after Behaviour Interactive gifted TheBurntPeanut every Dead by Daylight DLC during a livestream on October 30, sparking backlash from parts of the community.

The situation escalated after the game’s official account replied “WE CAN TAKE THE HEAT!” when the streamer offered to give the content back.

On November 3, Behaviour removed the gifted DLC, admitting the decision had skipped its usual internal checks and that it had “moved too fast.”

Dead by Daylight team respond following backlash to TheBurntPeanut receiving free DLC

Valve admits it wrongly slapped adults only Steam rating on indie vampire game

“This incident has broken trust with our community,” the developer said, adding that it is rewriting its creator guidelines and “taking appropriate action internally” with the staff involved.

XRP Taker Buy/Sell Ratio Nears Balance as Price Slips

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

Yash is a crypto analyst specializing in price analysis, predictions, and in-depth research reports. He combines technical indicators with on-chain data to uncover market trends and potential breakouts. His sharp insights help readers navigate the crypto market with confidence. Whether it’s Bitcoin or emerging altcoins, Yash breaks it down with clarity and precision.

XRP's 14-day Binance taker buy/sell ratio reached 0.998 on November 5, close to balance.

The ratio rose from 0.896 on November 2, while XRP fell from $1.25 to around $1.21.

Easing aggressive selling doesn't confirm a reversal; the possible impact of Binance's hack remains unverified.

XRP’s taker buy/sell ratio on Binance is moving toward balance, but the token’s price isn’t following suit. The 14-day simple moving average (SMA) reached 0.998 on November 5, when XRP traded at $1.206. That’s close to 1.0, suggesting aggressive buyers and sellers were trading at roughly equal volume.

It’s a notable shift. Just three days earlier, the ratio stood at 0.896. The seven-day average has also climbed to 0.976, pointing to easing selling pressure. Still, don’t mistake a move toward balance for a confirmed bullish reversal.

The price action tells a less encouraging story. XRP fell from $1.25 on November 2 to around $1.21 on November 5, even as the taker ratio improved.

In other words, aggressive selling on Binance appears to have eased, but buyers haven’t yet pushed the price higher. That distinction matters. A better ratio doesn’t automatically mean fresh demand has arrived.

Trading flows may also have been affected by the October 29 Binance hot-wallet hack, during which approximately $6.2 billion to $6.4 billion was stolen. Whether that event directly influenced XRP taker activity remains unverified.

The dollar index stood at 101.56, roughly unchanged over one month. However, this is a broader US-level measure, not a direct reading of XRP trading flows.

One possible explanation is that forced selling has cooled after recent declines. The Binance incident may also have influenced trading activity, but neither explanation is confirmed.

For now, the XRP taker buy/sell ratio offers a modest sign of easing pressure, not proof of a turnaround. The reading is based on a short window, and the claim that it reached its highest level since August still needs verification against the full historical series.

Assassins’s Creed Codename Mars Leaked, Set In Ancient Rome

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

We Might Be Waiting A Bit For Codename Mars

Almost immediately after we published this article, Insider Gaming shared a report backing up Codename Mars and reporting that Assassin's Creed Hexe has been internally delayed. With it now suggested to launch in 2028, that very likely means that the Ancient Rome game is even further down the line.

A reliable Ubisoft insider has reported that two new Assassin's Creed games are in the works, including Codename Mars that will be set in Ancient Rome.

Considering it wasn't that long ago that Ubisoft came out and revealed two massive mainline Assassin's Creed games in a row, a remake of one of the most popular entries, and a bunch of spinoffs, it's weird how little we currently know about the future of the series. All that's been outright confirmed is the awesome-sounding Assassin's Creed Hexe, the Netflix show, and some mysterious side projects.

Of course, there's no doubt that the studio is already hard at work on whatever big game in the series comes after Hexe and the recently wrapped-up Black Flag remake, but we don't have any idea what it could be. That's just changed thanks to a reliable Ubisoft insider who claims that two more games are in the works, including a mainline entry that sounds pretty massive.

As pointed out by Redditor Gyatto- on the GamingLeaksAndRumours subreddit, reliable Ubisoft and Assassin's Creed insider xj0nathan recently shared a video that details two upcoming games in the series. The first, and undeniably the biggest, is a project that goes by the codename "Mars", which a reliable source tells him is set in Ancient Rome.

Despite the weight of the world on its shoulders, Assassin's Creed Shadows is a great entry in the series with one of its best worlds to date.

The insider sadly doesn't have many more details on Codename Mars outside of the time period and project name, but it is interesting considering the Netflix series is confirmed to be set at the same time. It's not hard to imagine the two tying into each other a bit, and I can already picture a very similar experience to Odyssey.

Although this is the first time we'll be exploring Ancient Rome, Assassin's Creed Brotherhood was set there in the 1500s.

The other game that was revealed by xj0nathan is a mobile game set in Jerusalem called Assassin's Creed Pocket, which will apparently have similar gameplay to Valhalla's Mastery Challenges. While another smaller Assassin's Creed game isn't that exciting personally, the claim that it'll have similar visuals to Prince of Persia: The Lost Crown does make it a bit more interesting.

We finally got a chance to play The Legend of Zelda: Ocarina of Time remake and it's still one of the best games ever made.

Tarik Muharemovic is making Leeds feel like they did with Raphinha – but history can be rewritten

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

We’re not even a third of the way through the 2026-27 Premier League campaign, but we’re already getting the same feeling watching Tarik Muharemovic that we got watching Raphinha at Leeds United way back when.

Not quite the same feelings, admittedly. Different players, different roles.

As a centre-back by trade, it’d be borderline impossible for Muharemovic to evoke quite the same sensation that Raphinha gave us when he pulled off the most outrageous of nutmegs on Gary Cahill.

He’s a defender. He’s not going to leave jaws on the floor with outrageous tricks or moments of individual brilliance. But we’re almost every bit as impressed by what Muharemovic brings to the table.

You watch him and think: how have Leeds pulled this off? This is a player who surely belongs at the Champions League level, and it’s difficult to imagine that not being his eventual destination.

We recognised those same qualities in Raphinha remarkably early in his Leeds career, and look at him now; playing a key role in multiple La Liga title triumphs, producing Ballon d’Or-worthy campaigns, and currently sitting atop the European goalscoring charts.

Muharemovic might not quite hit those very same heights – again, he’s a centre-back – but you’ve watched him at Leeds and can’t escape the feeling that he’s destined for the very top. He is surely the signing of the summer at £34.1million from Sassuolo.

Leeds are among the Premier League’s most watertight defences this season, with only Brighton and Arsenal conceding fewer goals. Muharemovic has been instrumental in that achievement, the standout player in Daniel Farke’s squad.

He was especially immense in Leeds’ 1-0 win over Tottenham, earning man-of-the-match honours with 14 defensive contributions, the joint-most on the pitch. He made 13 clearances, won four of his five aerial duels and wasn’t dribbled past once.

He was equally composed in possession, completing 80% of his passes, including 10 line-breaking efforts. You’ll find fewer more complete centre-back performances in the Premier League this season.

Player of the Match Tarik Muharemović reflects on a hard-fought win as he collects his award 😊

🎙️ @jules_breach | 📺 Watch live on TNT Sports & HBO Max pic.twitter.com/C0gzToyopK

— Football on TNT Sports (@footballontnt) November 7, 2026

“He did a tackle right in front of the South Stand and the whole place erupted. I was thinking, ‘you’re going to go down well with this club,” former Leeds United manager Simon Grayson told BBC Sport before his best display yet.

“I’m not daft enough to think he doesn’t have aspirations to one day play for some of the biggest clubs in the world,

“But right now? Sometimes a player is at home with a certain club and I get that feeling with Muharemovic. I get the impression he loves Leeds, he loves the city and he loves the supporters.”

It was much the same with Raphinha. He seemed to revel in being a Leeds player, feeding off the atmosphere at Elland Road and giving as much back as he got.

Every supporter who does not follow a club at the very top of the food chain – Real Madrid fans, basically – will recognise that bittersweet, almost melancholic feeling of being blessed with such a player. You’re punching above your weight, mate; she’ll leave you for some dickhead with double your salary that drives an Audi.

But you’d have to be a particularly miserable soul not to enjoy this while it lasts. He’s only just arrived, committed to a five-year contract, and there’s absolutely no reason to start contemplating life without him already.

There’s no reason Leeds can’t aspire to be the club that meets Muharemovic’s potential. Perhaps they can grow together, with the defender helping take them towards his level. That’s the dream, anyway. What’s the point of football without dreams?

Ahead of Raphinha’s second season, Leeds signed Junior Firpo and Daniel James. An injury crisis followed, Marcelo Bielsa was sacked, Jesse Marsch took over and, by the time Barcelona came calling, the Brazilian was taking long throws. He had clearly outgrown that nonsense.

History doesn’t have to repeat itself. Leeds have recruited well under the 49ers, and there’s plenty to like about the club’s direction of travel. The squad needs strengthening, particularly in attack, but why can’t they keep pulling off masterstrokes like Muharemovic and build something capable of matching his ambitions?

The future can wait. Right now, Leeds have one of the Premier League’s finest defenders. That’s something to enjoy, not fret about.

Tesla deemed liable for fraud after Solar Roof ‘experiment’ on a home

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

A California judge has deemed Tesla liable for fraud in a Solar Roof lawsuit, after the company repeatedly failed to hand over evidence. Tesla had already admitted it breached the contract, broke its warranty, and was negligent on the job.

The homeowner says fixing the damage will cost “well over $2.5 million.” Tesla once offered him $5,000.

The case is Conroy v. Tesla, filed in 2022 in San Diego Superior Court’s North County division (case No. 37-2022-00043670). Electrek reviewed the court orders, the complaint, and the parties’ joint trial readiness report. Here’s what’s in them.

Ambrose Conroy, who lives in Rancho Santa Fe, signed up for a Tesla Solar Roof in March 2020. The original contract was $82,210 for a 13.1 kW system. By February 2021, after two amendments, it had grown to roughly $110,000 for an 18.2 kW roof and six Powerwalls.

The roof was never finished. It never passed a county inspection, and it never produced a single kWh for the house.

Tesla isn’t contesting most of that anymore. In the joint report filed by both sides last month, Tesla stipulated to liability on three of the five claims.

The other two claims, fraud and negligent misrepresentation, are where things went badly for Tesla.

Judge Cynthia Freeland sanctioned Tesla twice over discovery before going further. She ordered it to pay $15,467.50 in February and another $13,500 in June, both times for failing to produce a proper privilege log and responsive documents, and both times warning that issue sanctions could follow.

Tesla missed the next deadline anyway. So on September 11, the court deemed Tesla liable for fraud and negligent misrepresentation, limiting the trial on those claims to compensatory and punitive damages.

The judge found “ample evidence to support that Defendants have acted willfully.” Among the missing records were Microsoft Teams messages (Tesla’s own corporate witness testified they’re kept “for infinity”), OSHA logs, emails with San Diego County inspectors, and more than 20 quality, install, and safety reports from Tesla’s internal Bolt system.

Tesla asked her to reconsider, arguing it had since produced everything. She said no in a ruling dated October 27, writing that the late documents could support Conroy’s theory that Tesla “knowingly used Plaintiff’s home as an experiment while knowing they lacked the experience to perform as promised.”

And the “experiment” word shows up in Tesla’s own messages, according to the trial exhibit list. Exhibit 224 is described as text messages from January 13, 2021, between Tesla’s director of model engineering Bobby Yang and other Tesla employees.

To be clear, that’s how Conroy’s side describes the messages on the exhibit list. We haven’t seen the texts themselves. But Elon Musk is on Conroy’s witness list, and internal emails quoted in the complaint point the same way.

On February 9, 2021, Yang wrote internally that the house was in the “99.5 percentile in terms of complexity” and that “if we can do this job well, we should be able to do any job well.” RJ Johnson, then head of Tesla’s energy business, replied that the project would “skew numbers” but that Tesla needed “to make it right.”

Johnson was fired in April 2021. The roof had at least 59 mounting planes, and one employee called it the largest job Tesla had attempted at the time, according to the complaint.

Conroy’s account of the installation reads like a list of everything that can go wrong on a roof. These are his allegations, but Tesla has now conceded breach and negligence.

The tear-off was supposed to take two days and took two weeks. Tesla left in November 2020 with bare roof decking exposed, then had crews work overtime on December 27 to get underlayment down before a storm. Tiles arrived scratched and damaged and went on the roof anyway. Glass tiles shattered in the driveway and lawn, and the family says they still can’t walk there barefoot.

By March 2021, Johnson had agreed in writing that paying “when you’re happy with the outcome is completely reasonable.” Tesla’s billing department kept calling anyway.

In August 2021, an internal email weighed just leaving.

The project went through at least seven project managers. In November 2021, Tesla told Conroy the people behind its repair plan were gone and it had nobody in the area to do the work.

In April 2022, Tesla offered $5,000 to settle everything.

It didn’t end there. In February 2025, more than two years into the lawsuit, Tesla failed another San Diego County inspection after showing up without the plans for the venting system. And this summer, Conroy’s electrical expert opened up part of the roof and concluded that the way Tesla ran wiring and conduit under the tiles created a short-circuit and fire risk in heavy rain. The roof is now coming off. Tesla’s lawyers point out that even Conroy’s counsel called that fire risk “counterintuitive.”

Tesla is fighting the sanction hard. In an October 29 filing, it asked the court to freeze the case while it petitions the California Court of Appeal, arguing the order is “unduly punitive,” violates due process, and amounts to a directed verdict on fraud before trial.

Tesla also says the discovery fight had no connection to the fraud claims and that it has now produced everything relevant. The judge already rejected that argument once.

The November 20 trial date is off. A trial readiness conference is set for December 4, and Conroy’s lawyers want to go to trial in December.

This isn’t the first Solar Roof horror story in this case file. Conroy’s complaint attaches our 2020 report on a Solar Roof buyer left with tarps over his house after Tesla tried an untested underlayment on his home. It also cites a 2025 California Contractors State License Board action seeking to revoke Tesla’s contractor licenses over abandoned Solar Roof jobs.

What a nightmare. This is the most damaging Solar Roof case I’ve seen, and I’ve been covering this product since Elon Musk unveiled it on a Universal Studios backlot in 2016.

A botched install isn’t news on its own. Tesla only installed about 3,000 of these roofs before killing the product this year, and plenty of them went sideways. What’s different here is the paper trail: internal emails calling a customer’s home a test of whether Tesla could do “any job,” texts describing the job as an “experiment” with daily reports going to Musk, and then years of Tesla failing to hand over its own records until a judge ran out of patience.

And Tesla’s response to all of it was $5,000.

Look, it’s normal for things not to go as planned with a new product, and early adopters need to be flexible. However, we are talking about someone’s literal roof over their head here and a trillion-dollar company. Tesla could have and should have made it right a long time ago.

I suspect that by the time this is all said and done, how many ever years that is from now, it would be cheaper for Tesla to hand over a 2.5M$ check to the family. This feels like it's going long battle and they've already started spending on lawyers with more to come. And even if by some miracle Tesla wins the fraud part, they've already agreed to breach of contract, etc which is going to cost them significantly.

The issue sanction might not survive the Court of Appeal. Courts don’t love deciding fraud through discovery orders, and Tesla’s due process argument isn’t frivolous. But even if it gets reversed, Tesla has still admitted it breached the contract and was negligent, and the jury will see how this customer was treated.

This also says a lot about how Tesla ran the Solar Roof business. It sold a product it knew was hard to install, on homes it knew were complex, while the people running the program kept leaving. Conroy’s house was one of the worst cases, but installers are still dealing with the fallout of Tesla walking away from the product earlier this year.

The real question is what’s in those Teams messages and texts Tesla fought so hard to keep out of this case. If this goes to trial in December, we might find out.

Solar Roof was supposed to make going solar effortless. For most homeowners, conventional panels from a reputable local installer are the safer bet. With electricity rates up almost 10% last year and expected to keep climbing, going solar is one of the best ways to protect yourself against rising costs. And with lease and PPA options, you can do it with zero upfront cost and start saving immediately. If you want to find the best deal, check out EnergySage. AI Chip Stocks Bounce After Global Rout—But Is the Bubble Starting to Burst?

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

AI Chip Stocks Bounce After Global Rout—But Is the Bubble Starting to Burst?

AI stocks staged a comeback Friday after a rout that swept from Wall Street to Asia. But a single green session hardly settles the bigger question for investors heading into the weekend: Is this an overdue correction, or the beginning of an AI bubble bursting?

Nvidia rose 2.78% to $186.64, after plunging 9.3% this Thursday. AMD also bounced to $449.04 following a 17.3% collapse a day earlier. Both stocks remain well below their October peaks, showing how little a sharp relief rally can repair.

It wasn’t just U.S. markets seeing red either. South Korea’s KOSPI lost 6% Friday, while Taiwan’s benchmark dropped more than 8% during the week—its worst week in months. TSMC hit its daily limit-down threshold during the rout. Reuters reported that Taiwanese officials asked state-owned banks to help support prices.

The US Tech 100 cash-market proxy also recovered, gaining 1.85% to 27,007.3, which means it’s down more than 5.5% this week. Its daily candles show the bounce arriving after a much larger slide from October levels, not a return to the previous trend.

The U.S. midterms accelerated fears that Democratic gains would mean tougher scrutiny of data centers. In 40 of 41 local ballot contests on November 3, voters favored limiting development, including by rejecting pro-construction measures, Ballotpedia found. Some votes were advisory, but the political message was unmistakable.

That matters for chip companies because data centers are their biggest growth story. Each project requires vast spending on processors, servers, power and cooling. Restrictions could delay orders or force construction elsewhere.

Yet the doubts predated the election. AI companies and their customers are spending aggressively to build capacity, often before returns are clear. More expensive borrowing makes that bet harder to sustain. Reuters quoted market strategist Steve Sosnick warning that heavily owned stocks were already “priced to perfection.”

A correction means a market has fallen at least 10% from a recent peak. It does not necessarily mean the underlying business is shrinking. Imagine a chipmaker whose shares trade at 60 times annual earnings because investors expect explosive growth. If investors become willing to pay only 40 times those same earnings, the stock can lose a third of its value without profits falling at all.

A bubble bursting is more serious: It happens when expectations prove unrealistic and the money supporting the boom begins to disappear.

History offers a useful warning. During the dot-com crash, the Nasdaq lost roughly 77% between March 2000 and October 2002. The internet was not a fad, but investors had vastly overestimated how quickly companies could turn it into profit.

Telecom operators spent fortunes laying fiber-optic cables for customers who had yet to arrive. Global Crossing, which built an international network, filed for bankruptcy in 2002. The technology survived; many companies and shareholders did not.

AI could face a similar mismatch between long-term promise and near-term returns—and some investors are already talking about it. For example, the RAM shortage is also a business opportunity that many companies are trying to use for their advantage. Former crypto miners are renting their hardware and cloud provider and inference startups are appearing everywhere.

Now, consider a hypothetical company borrowing billions to build a data center packed with Nvidia chips, expecting to rent that computing power at premium prices for years. If customers spend less than anticipated, borrowing becomes more expensive or construction is delayed, the debt still has to be repaid.

The operator may cancel chip orders, its contractors lose work, and utilities that planned new capacity lose anticipated customers. Lenders and investors holding its debt could also take losses. A stock-market selloff can then become a problem for the wider economy.

There is an important difference from 2000, however. Microsoft, Alphabet, Amazon and Meta are profitable businesses with established customers and substantial cash flows, unlike many of the speculative dot-com startups. They can withstand setbacks better than smaller AI-cloud providers that depend on repeatedly borrowing to expand.

It is possible for AI to keep transforming industries while a bunch of individual AI companies fail—and for investors in successful companies to have overpaid.

The financial warning lights are beginning to flicker. Reuters reported Friday that the cost of insuring against a default by AI-cloud provider CoreWeave had climbed above 1,000 basis points. Such protection works somewhat like insurance on a loan: as the price rises, investors are signaling greater concern about repayment.

Yet broader bond markets were still operating relatively normally. That points to growing strain in the riskiest corners of AI financing, not proof of a system-wide crisis.

Next week will offer more concrete tests than another day of red or green candles. Results from CoreWeave, Applied Materials, and Cisco could reveal whether customers are still ordering capacity and equipment, while inflation data will influence expectations for borrowing costs. Stable orders and manageable debt would support the correction argument.

Repeated canceled projects, failed refinancing and shrinking sales would suggest the boom's financial foundations are cracking. The question is not whether AI has a future, but whether every company betting billions on that future can afford to get there.

Dogecoin Contributor Flags Social Signing as Potential Wallet Security Risk

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

Disclaimer: The opinions expressed by our writers are their own and do not represent the views of U.Today. The financial and market information provided on U.Today is intended for informational purposes only. U.Today is not liable for any financial losses incurred while trading cryptocurrencies. Conduct your own research by contacting financial experts before making any investment decisions. We believe that all content is accurate as of the date of publication, but certain offers mentioned may no longer be available.

Dogecoin community member Mishaboar has raised concerns about the security risks associated with social signing, a feature that allows users to create and access a blockchain wallet using everyday accounts like Google, Apple ID, Telegram, or email instead of managing a complex seed phrase.

This comes after Keplr, a multichain wallet supporting Cosmos, Bitcoin, Ethereum, and Starknet, stated it had received reports of unauthorized transactions on some Keplr accounts created with Google or Apple login.

The wallet provider stated that the incident has been escalated to the Web3Auth team, with both working together to investigate the matter.

Before Keplr's disclosure, Web3 wallet infrastructure provider Web3Auth said it was aware of a security issue affecting Web3Auth's services used by some third parties. It stated that it had temporarily paused the embedded wallet service in response to the issue; however, after an initial investigation, no evidence was found that MetaMask users were impacted.

In his X post, Mishaboar criticized social signing. "Social signing, the feature you may want to add to your wallet if you hate your users," Mishaboar wrote.

Social signing, the feature you may want to add to your wallet if you hate your users. The worst part is that the users that use the social signing option -and which will end up being hacked through it - are also often newbies, entering crypto for the first time. And the risk goes beyond the funds in your wallet - data leaks could link your real life identity to your holdings, which can have terrible consequences.

The concern is particularly significant for inexperienced crypto users who may not recognize the risks associated with connecting their personal accounts to wallet services. While the feature makes wallet creation and access easier, it could introduce security vulnerabilities that users may not fully understand, Mishaboar argued.

"The worst part is that the users that use the social signing option—and who will end up being hacked through it—are also often newbies, entering crypto for the first time. And the risk goes beyond the funds in your wallet—data leaks could link your real-life identity to your holdings, which can have terrible consequences," Mishaboar stated.

Mishaboar noted that this warning also extends to wallets using social sign-in, which allows users to back up their keys in the "Cloud," which just means somebody else's server, he explained.

This week, a security advisory was issued for iOS users, who are urged to update their devices to enhance their wallet security. This follows security researchers identifying two exploit kits, Coruna and DarkSword, which target devices running older iOS versions. These do not rely on a flaw in any wallet app, but once a device is compromised, they can access wallet data stored on it.

This Focus RS Makes Us Nostalgic for a Time When Ford Built Cars Besides the Mustang

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

This hot hatch has just 2200 miles and is being sold by the original owner on Bring a Trailer.

Ford killed off the Focus globally just last year, much to the chagrin of anyone who enjoyed a scrappy little hatchback or sedan with a blue oval up front. Sure, the Mustang is still around, but the Focus and the Fiesta provided an entirely different kind of Fast Ford, compact performance that you could use every day. And there was one Focus trim that was the best of them all.

Here’s a nearly new example of maximum Focus: a 2016 Focus RS with just 2200 miles on the odometer, for sale on Bring a Trailer (which, like Car and Driver, is part of Hearst Autos). It’s a single-owner, garage-kept car from Texas, and for those in colder climes, it happens to have been optioned with the RS2 package that includes heated seats and a heated steering wheel. Slap on a set of snow tires, and get ready to shred this upcoming winter.

See all results for used 2016 Ford Focus RS for sale near 66502

Pairing a 350-hp 2.3-liter turbocharged four-cylinder engine with a six-speed manual gearbox and clever all-wheel drive, the Focus RS was basically Ford’s answer to the Subaru STI and the Mitsubishi Lancer EVO. WRC champion Colin McRae did spend quite a bit of time rallying in Fords, and that heritage was baked right into the RS, even if U.S. fans weren’t as broadly familiar with the world of rally.

For those who were, the RS was a revelation. The punchy four built boost quickly, and it somehow had far more turbo personality than when it appeared in the EcoBoost version of the Mustang. The all-wheel drive was even better, equipped with the ability to direct as much as 70 percent of the power to either outside rear wheel. With understeer thus banished, the RS could tear up a winding back road with a pace equal to far more expensive machines.

And yes, it came with Drift mode. Fully unlock this ability by switching off traction control, and the Focus RS can lay down Ken Block–style tire-smoking slides with ease. Not great for your tire budget, but huge fun nonetheless.

The only drawback was trying to get your hands on one when new, as dealers often tried to add considerable markup to the price tag. This example, painted a laid-back Stealth Gray, flies under the radar a bit and is just waiting for an owner who is going to pack on the miles.

In a low-key color, and with only hints of the performance peeking out from that hatchback shape in the form of a rear wing and blue Brembo brakes, this RS is a bit of an if-you-know-then-you-know car. It’s a real shame that Ford doesn’t sell anything like it anymore. On the other hand, that just makes this one that much more special.

Women often get cold more easily than men — a mouse study offers hints why

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

A new study points to testosterone and proteins in the skin as reasons why males and females experience temperatures differently.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

It's a common observation that women often prefer warmer temperatures and feel cold more easily than men do, and the same trend appears among females of other species. Now, a study offers new clues as to what drives this preference — and testosterone may play a part.

Skin carries sensors to detect heat and cold that influence each organism's decision to stay in or move out of an environment. The new study, published Oct. 28 in the Journal of Physiology, found that these sensors activate at higher temperatures in female mice, which appears to shift their preferred temperature roughly 7 degrees Fahrenheit (4 degrees Celsius) higher than that of male mice. Testosterone seems to influence this difference in activation.

"You could say it's just a cute observation of no particular importance," said study co-author Peter McNaughton, a pharmacologist at King's College London who studies pain, thermal sensation and thermoregulation. "[But] it's got practical ramifications," in that it might be important in designing buildings and spaces that accommodate people, he told Live Science.

Proteins in the skin's nerve endings activate in response to warmth or cold. "You can look at them like little electrical generators in the nerve endings," said Dr. Michael Caterina, a neuroscientist at Johns Hopkins University who was not involved in the research.

When activated, these proteins allow charged ions to enter sensory nerve cells, which, in turn, transmit electrical signals to the brain. The circuits a given nerve cell connects to determines our perception of heat or cold, Caterina told Live Science.

Beyond humans, it is known that many female animals, including mice and birds, prefer slightly warmer temperatures than their male counterparts do. So the researchers set up an experiment to investigate these differences.

They put 30 male and 27 female mice on two adjacent plates of different temperatures — one at 91.4 degrees Fahrenheit (33 degrees Celsius) and another that varied between lower and higher temperatures. A camera tracked the mice's movements and calculated how much time they spent on each plate.

Get the world’s most fascinating discoveries delivered straight to your inbox.

Both male and female mice spent more time at the 91.4 F plate when the second plate was cooler. However, when put next to a plate measuring 100.4 F (38 C), female mice spent significantly more time on the warmer plate than male mice did. The average preferred surface temperature was approximately 89.6 F (32 C) for male mice and 96.8 F (36 C) for female mice.

"Since the top of the cage was completely open, there was no question of the air temperature affecting the mice," McNaughton said, so the preference was driven solely by the sensors in their skin. The researchers also measured the internal and skin temperatures of the mice and confirmed that there were no significant differences between the sexes on those fronts.

The scientists explored what would happen if they genetically deleted the warmth-detector protein. In male mice, this reduced their avoidance of the warmer plate, suggesting this protein normally sends a signal that discourages them from staying on warmer surfaces. The mice's preferred temperature also rose to about 95 F (35 C), closer to that of females.

The study suggests that testosterone somehow tweaks the function of heat-sensing nerves in the skin.

In female mice, deleting or blocking the protein did not have any significant effect.

The team then tried eliminating or blocking the coolness-detecting protein, finding that males and females didn't avoid the cool plates as strongly as they did before. Female mice also spent less time on the warmer plates, but males' preference for the warmer plates didn't change.

"Females are a little bit more responsive to activation of the cold receptor," Caterina said, "and a little bit less responsive to activation of the warmer receptor." That's despite the temperature-sensing neurons in both sets of mice working identically, the team found.

The researchers suspected that sex hormones might help to explain this difference.

They gave a group of male mice a drug that blocks the production of testosterone. Weeks after the treatment, these mice spent more time on the 38 C plate than males with normal testosterone. Meanwhile, female mice given testosterone lost their preference for warmer temperatures.

Interestingly, giving male mice female hormones, including forms of estrogen and progesterone, did not affect their temperature preferences. And blocking the hormones in female mice also had no effect. These hormones are known to influence other aspects of temperature regulation in the body, though, such as heat dissipation through the skin; but in this case, they didn't seem to affect animals' perception of temperature.

"So it really seems as though testosterone is the most important determinant," McNaughton said. But these changes were only partial; testosterone did not completely reverse the differences between the sexes. "So there is something else going on that we weren't able to identify," he added.

The exact mechanism for how testosterone impacts the sensor proteins is not clear. Because the effect happened over weeks, researchers suggested that it did not directly affect the temperature-sensing proteins. It might instead act indirectly by influencing the activity of genes, but further studies would be needed to confirm this idea.

Although the researchers did not conduct any tests in humans, McNaughton noted that the same sensor proteins are present in people and that studies with humans suggest that they behave in a similar way.

This research provides "valuable information" for understanding how the proteins responsible for sensing temperature work, Caterina said. Understanding these mechanisms could be useful for studying conditions like neuropathic pain, in which people's sensitivity to heat and cold is enhanced.

"What kinds of signals create our daily experiences around temperature is by itself very important," he told Live Science. "It's a big part of understanding ourselves as living beings."

This article is for informational purposes only and is not meant to offer medical advice.

Vilar, B., & McNaughton, P. A. (2026). TRPM8 and TRPM2 ion channels determine sex differences in thermal preference in mice. The Journal of Physiology. https://doi.org/10.1113/jp288076

Zunnash Khan is a mechatronics engineer and a science journalist from Pakistan. She has written for Science, The Scientist and Brainfacts.org, among other outlets.

Danny Murphy on why you won’t see him on Strictly Come Dancing

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

The former Liverpool man insists he won’t be adding to the ranks of ex-pros on the primetime BBC show

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

For two decades, Danny Murphy enjoyed a hugely successful playing career, winning a host of major honours with Liverpool and earning nine England caps.

Since hanging up his boots in 2013, the former Crewe Alexandra academy graduate has become a familiar face in the media, making regular appearances for the BBC on television and radio.

And while Murphy has found his groove as a pundit, there are some broadcasting avenues that he has no desire to go down.

Ratings-behemoth Strictly Come Dancing is one of the BBC’s most popular shows and has seen plenty of sports stars swap the playing field for the ballroom in recent years.

Former Lionesses star Karen Carney became the first ex-footballer to claim the glitterball last year, while Shaun Wright-Phillips bowed out five weeks into the current series.

But any fans wanting to see Murphy swap the commentary box for the sequins and become the latest ex-player to strut his stuff on the dancefloor will be left disappointed.

“I’ve turned Strictly down twice,” Murphy tells FourFourTwo. “I enjoy a good dance, but I’m not into sequins and I’ve got a busy life – it had nothing to do with money.”

The best features, fun and footballing quizzes, straight to your inbox every week.

For some sporting personalities, appearing on Strictly has been a way to show off a different side of themselves, but Murphy is content to leave this particular challenge to others.

Instead, the ex-Fulham and Tottenham man simply knows where his interests lie and does not feel the need to step out of his comfort zone and into the Strictly limelight.

“For me, the less limelight, the better,” he continues. “I love talking about football, it’s the one area in life that I feel I know something about.”

This preference for sticking to his strengths is why Murphy rejects the idea of taking on an entirely new challenge in front of millions of viewers.

“Crossing over to do something that isn’t my strong point? It’s not for me,” he concludes.

For more than a decade, Joe Mewis has worked in football journalism as a reporter and editor. Mewis has had stints at Mirror Football and LeedsLive among others and worked at FourFourTwo throughout Euro 2024, reporting on the tournament. In addition to his journalist work, Mewis is also the author of four football history books that include times on Leeds United and the England national team. Now working as a digital marketing coordinator at Harrogate Town, too, Mewis counts some of his best career moments as being in the iconic Spygate press conference under Marcelo Bielsa and seeing his beloved Leeds lift the Championship trophy during lockdown.

Hunting the ‘Old Ripper’ Was Almost More Adventure Than I Bargained For

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

Published Nov 7, 2026 4:00 PM EST

Get the hottest outdoor news from Outdoor Life.

I had just come back from the service in 1954. My wife was expecting our first baby, and I was running a general store on Highway 97 just outside of Bainbridge, Georgia. Hardly a day went by that someone didn’t come in and tell tales of Old Ripper.

Old Ripper was a large black wild hog that lived in the nearby Fowltown Swamp. That hog tore up more fences, broke more gates, broke the backs of more female hogs and was chased by more hunters than any critter there has ever been in this country. Ripper had been shot by several people. My Daddy fired at him seven times with an M-1 carbine and believed he hit him, but the hog never fell. He had been hunted with dogs and he had killed plenty of dogs. There never was a hog hound that could hold him. The last time the boar was run with hounds, he killed two of the best hounds in the county with his slashing tusks, so no one else ever put their dogs on Old Ripper.

Old Ripper got his name because there seemed to be absolutely nothing he couldn’t rip up or utterly destroy. Farmers around Bainbridge went to the hardware stores and bought brand-new aluminum gates to put in the gaps he made in their fences. Within a week, the gates were often all bent and torn up where that crazy boar had stuck his nose under and bent them to get into a pasture or field. There was no hog wire or barbed wire fence that could keep Old Ripper out.

That boar was walking destruction. The farmers hated him not only for tearing up fences and gates, but even more because of what he did to their sows. Ripper would break into a pig parlor, tear up the pens and release all the sows. Then he would take them off for a stroll in the woods. Old Ripper weighed 438 pounds. So when he mounted some of the young sows, he broke their backs. Then the sows would have to be destroyed. The sows he did breed threw shoats that were as wild and crazy as he was. The piglets wouldn’t fatten and brought almost nothing at the market.

Everybody was out to get Old Ripper. My dad was accustomed to catching wild hogs. We used to catch them in the Fowltown Swamp, fatten them and sell them at the market. My daddy knew about trapping hogs. He built traps to catch Old Ripper. On two separate occasions Old Ripper did go into one of daddy’s traps, but he tore up the wooden stockades and broke out both times. My daddy wouldn’t stand for that, so daddy got a steel bear trap and set it for Ripper. When daddy went back the next day he found the giant tracks of Old Ripper and followed them to the trap. But instead of catching a renegade hog, he found the bear trap had been sprung by the big boar.

One reason Old Ripper became a legend was his ability to outthink the farmers and the hunters in our area. He had been around humans so much that in many cases he just outthought folks instead of using brute force. The people around our place began to think Old Ripper might be a supernatural creature. He was a boar, but he was a smart boar. No dogs could hold him, no stockades could enclose him, no fence could stop him and no man had been able to kill him for seven years.

Other episodes added to the legend of Old Ripper. One of the meanest things Old Ripper ever did involved Mrs. Ella T. Hand’s daughter-in-law, who was five months pregnant. She was in her kitchen and heard a disturbance out among her sows. She went out to the pig parlor and saw Old Ripper trying to tear the planks off the posts where she had her sows penned up. She picked up a rock and threw it at the old boar to try and run him off. When he saw her, he turned, looked straight at her and charged. Only a few steps away was a giant red oak stump that stood about three feet off the ground. The lady climbed up on the stump and began to scream for her husband. All the while, Old Ripper was going around the stump, snorting and blowing. He attempted to rear up on that stump and get to her but she stayed just out of reach of his razor-sharp tusks. For an hour the hog circled the stump as the lady screamed for help. Finally her husband came home. When he saw what was happening, he grabbed his shotgun from over the fireplace and ran toward the hog. At less than 10 yards, he shot Old Ripper in the side of the head with birdshot. The hog merely shook his head, looked at the man and trotted off.

To say Old Ripper was hated by everybody in the community would be a vast understatement. Every man and boy dreamed of killing that old boar, and plenty of work was forgotten by the men who hunted him. All it took to organize a community hog hunt was for someone to report that they’d seen Old Ripper. Every man and boy within earshot would grab a gun and head for the swamp.

I would have liked to have hunted Old Ripper, but I had mouths to feed. I didn’t have time to waste on that renegade boar. I had just bought a brand-new lever-action Marlin .30/30. I was proud of that gun and took it out behind the store to test it out. On that particular day, Mrs. Ella T. Hand happened to be sitting out in front of the store. Mrs. Hand was close to 80 years old. She always wore a sunbonnet, a long dress and about 90 percent of the time, she had a dip of snuff in her mouth. Mrs. Hand saw me kill a crow sitting in the field about 150 yards away with that rifle. I knew I’d made a lucky shot, but she didn’t. She told everyone for a month about how good I could shoot with that new .30/30.

“If that old wild hog comes back around my sows, I’m going to come and get you, Jack, and let you kill him with that new rifle,” she told me.

Well, about a month later, here comes Mrs. Ella T. in her 1949 pickup truck.

“Jack, that old boar is up in my field with some sows he run off,” she told me. “Now I don’t expect you’ll have any better luck than anybody else who has tried to kill him, but I sure wish you’d bring that new rifle up to the field and give it a try.”

I closed up my little country store and got into the front seat of that pickup truck with Mrs. Ella T. to try my hand at Old Ripper. I had five cartridges with copper-jacketed lead-headed bullets in my gun—the kind that open up but still go on through. There wasn’t any doubt in my mind that if I could hit that boar, the bullets would do the damage needed to put him down.

We drove to Mrs. Hand’s 40-acre peanut field, which backed up to the edge of Fowltown Swamp. I saw Old Ripper out in the middle of the field with four sows he had just broken out from their pen. The five pigs were all rooting in the peanuts. There was a hog wire fence around the field, with barbed wire on the top. The field, which was about 250 yards wide and about twice as long, was on a hillside. Old Ripper and the sows were up close to the top of the hill and I was down near the bottom. There was a dried-up creekbed that ran under the fence. From the tracks in the wash, I figured this was where Old Ripper and the sows had gotten into the field. The creekbed was only about two or three feet deep, but it was big enough for me to crawl under the fence and still stay out of sight. I could lay down in the bottom of the wash, crawl along on my stomach and Old Ripper wouldn’t be able to see me.

Until that day, I had never seen Old Ripper. I had looked at fences he had torn up and I had seen mud on the sides of the trees where he had rubbed. I knew he was a big boar, but I never realized just how big. When I saw Old Ripper standing there in the sun, his black bristles gathered in the rays of light and made him look a deep royal blue. He was a magnificent animal standing about four feet high, and he completely dwarfed the sows following him.

I said to myself, “Doggone your hide, Old Ripper! I’m going to try and get a little closer to you.”

The wash meandered from the bottom of the slope where I was uphill to where Old Ripper was rooting in the peanut patch. Lying flat on my belly, I would crawl for 10 yards and then peep over the edge to see where Old Ripper was. Each time I looked, the old boar was about 10 yards farther from the place than he had been before. It was just like he had a sixth sense that warned him of danger. He kept the same distance away, about 230 yards from me, from the time I first saw him until I decided to shoot. After I had crawled 30 yards, Old Ripper and his band of runaway sows were within 10 yards of a thick broom sedge field. I knew that if he got into that field, I wouldn’t be able to see to shoot him.

The wash was shallower and just barely kept me out of the boar’s view. The shot had to be over 200 yards. So I laid the rifle down on the lip of the wash, looked through the open sights and aimed at the top of Old Ripper’s shoulder. When I squeezed off the first shot, I saw the dust fly off the boar’s shoulder and heard the bullet make a dull thud when it hit him, but Old Ripper showed no sign of being wounded.

Then I stood up. Old Ripper reared up on his hind legs and bellowed like a wild bull. The hair on the back of my neck stood up. I had caught or killed more than 100 hogs out of that swamp, but I had never heard a hog make a sound like that.

When Old Ripper came down on his front feet, he was on a dead run, headed right down that hill. I knew that when he got to the bottom of the hill, he had business with me. I should have killed Old Ripper with that first shot, but he was far from dead. I didn’t panic. I knew that before the boar got to me, I would have time to fire four more of those .30/30 shells. I thought there was no way I wouldn’t kill him before he got to me. I had faced other wild hogs before.

With every shot, I believed I was pumping more lead into Old Ripper, but he never slowed. He still came on toward me. I took my time, aimed, squeezed, heard the report of the rifle, jacked another shell into the chamber and fired again. I knew I was hitting the boar, but he never missed a stride.

When Old Ripper was less than 50 yards away, I heard the hammer fall on an empty chamber. That was the loudest, most desperate sound I ever heard in my life.

I looked around and saw one lone cherry tree with no limbs on it about 30 yards to my right, but my feet wouldn’t work. I knew I couldn’t stay where I was, but I couldn’t go anywhere else. Old Ripper just kept on coming. I finally threw that gun down and began to run. Then I saw Old Ripper, now 20 yards away, running on two crippled front legs. I knew then that I had a chance to outrun him, but just as that idea went through my mind, the big boar fell and rolled to a stop about 10 yards away from me. He never moved, he never grunted and he never made another sound.

There was a slight breeze coming from that dead boar to me. Old Ripper’s odor was the strongest, rankest smell I ever have smelled. It was awful. I waited awhile to make sure Old Ripper was dead. Then I looked to see where my bullets had hit. The first had entered the shoulder. The second went through the hog’s loin and had gone into his stomach. Two other shots had hit his fore shoulders and entered the chest cavity. One bullet just flat-out missed.

Looking at Old Ripper, I was kind of sad that he was gone. Even if he was a renegade, he was a magnificent animal. There had never been a boar like him before that I ever heard tell of, and there never was another.

Mrs. Ella T.’s son James and her husband Ressie helped me load Old Ripper onto the pickup truck. We took the boar to Mr. Smoke Roger’s platform scales at Fayetteville, Georgia. The boar tipped the scales at 438 pounds. We struggled to reload the boar and then drove back to Bainbridge. Dr. Cooper, the veterinarian at that time, examined him. Below Ripper’s inch-thick hide was a sheath of gristle that extended from his huge shoulders to the middle of his back. This sheath had been Old Ripper’s armor and had turned the shotgun pellets and rifle bullets over the years. Dr. Cooper said the boar was between 8 and 10 years old and was probably a throwback to some of the European wild boars let loose in this country years before.

Read Next: I’ve Been Charged by Bears and Moose. Wild Hogs Are Much, Much Worse

When my daddy saw that the boar filled up the bed of that pickup truck, he could hardly believe his eyes. He had wanted to be the one who killed Old Ripper, but since he didn’t, he was glad it was me.

“That ol’ hog has cost farmers in this area thousands of dollars and many good sows, as well as being responsible for a lot of little pigs that weren’t fit to sell,” my daddy said. “I’m glad he’s gone.”

Since then the old Fowltown Swamp where Old Ripper used to roam and where wild pigs lived and bred for hundreds of years has vanished. Old Ripper’s haunts are covered by Lake Seminole today, but I remember it like it was when that boar was less than 30 yards away on a dead run bringing hurt to me, and I heard the sound of the hammer on my .30/30 falling on an empty chamber.
